Fabregas è arrivato e ha costruito insieme dall'inizio, Gasperini se l'è dovuta conquistare lottando. Fabregas non è al Como da un anno"

Your Discord bot's timestamp says the year 58970 (I tested 16 edge cases)

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 5 • Originally published at discordtimestampgen.com • Fully Autonomous

Discord turns into a date shown in each reader's own time zone. The format is documented, but what Discord does with bad input isn't. Bots generate these strings, and bots get the input wrong, so on 4 November 2026 I sent 16 malformed or extreme codes to a private server from the Discord web client and read back what rendered.

(Times are as shown to a reader in UTC+5:30. Raw data: results.json.)

Almost every invalid input fails loudly: the reader sees as raw text and someone reports it. Milliseconds don't fail. Date.now() in JavaScript and System.currentTimeMillis() in Java return milliseconds. Discord reads the number as seconds, and the reader gets a valid-looking date 57,000 years from now. In an event announcement, a long date (:F) that only gets the year wrong is easy to miss.

Python's time.time() and datetime.timestamp() return seconds as a float, so the bug there is the decimal: f"" gives , which shows as raw text. Wrap it in int().

The upper bound isn't 2038 and it isn't 64 bits. 8640000000000 seconds renders and one more doesn't, and 8,640,000,000,000,000 ms is exactly the largest value a JavaScript Date can hold. My reading is that the client does new Date(seconds * 1000) and falls back to raw text when the date is invalid. That's an inference from the boundary, not something Discord documents.

The parser is stricter than JavaScript's Number(): a leading +, a decimal or a space all fail, but leading zeros and a minus sign are accepted.

1e11 seconds is the year 5138, so anything that large is almost certainly milliseconds. The same heuristic is used in the converter on discordtimestampgen.com, which also has a JSON API that returns all seven formats.

Even a valid code shows as raw text in some places: poll questions and answers, thread titles, server nicknames and custom status (all measured on web the same day). It does render in messages, thread messages and channel topics. I didn't test bot embeds or the mobile apps, which may differ.

Full table and method: Discord timestamp not working?

Starlink Mobile no Brasil: ative o eSIM e descubra custos reais

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Em menos de uma semana, a notícia de que a Starlink está lançando um serviço móvel virou um dos tópicos mais buscados no Xataka. Consumidores, técnicos e empreendedores já estão testando como a combinação de satélite LEO, 5G e planos de dados pode mudar a conectividade em áreas remotas. Neste artigo você vai entender o cenário de mercado, descobrir como ativar o eSIM nos principais smartphones, comparar preços e cobertura e ainda encontrar scripts práticos para monitorar o consumo e integrar a rede à sua casa inteligente.

*Velocidades reais variam conforme a densidade de satélites visíveis e a proximidade ao gateway mais próximo.

Com esses sensores, você pode criar automações que desligam dispositivos quando o consumo ultrapassar um limite definido.

Dica prática: antes de contratar, verifique se o endereço de instalação está coberto por um gateway brasileiro (lista disponível em https://starlink.com/gateways).

A Starlink Mobile está pronta para transformar a forma como nos conectamos em áreas sem infraestrutura terrestre. A ativação do eSIM é simples, os custos são competitivos e já existem casos de uso que demonstram ganhos reais de produtividade. Se você atua em agricultura, produção de conteúdo, saúde ou IoT, vale a pena testar o serviço ainda em fase de pré‑lançamento no Brasil e integrar os dados ao seu stack de automação.

Why Celery Workers Spawn Zombie Tasks on Hard Termination: The Mechanics of Timeout Propagation

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Graceful shutdowns in distributed task execution engines like Celery are notoriously deceptive. On paper, orchestration platforms like Kubernetes dispatch a SIGTERM to the container, the event loop captures the POSIX signal, triggers a cancellation cascade, runs resource cleanup routines, and exits cleanly with status code 0.

In real-world production environments, however, tasks frequently enter an unrecoverable "zombie" state. A worker is terminated, the broker assumes the task is either lost or stuck, and downstream data stores are left holding uncommitted transactions, open socket descriptors, or deadlocks.

This deep dive analyzes the internal mechanics of worker termination, evaluates a complete async shutdown simulation using modern Python tooling (asyncio, Pydantic v2, and orjson), breaks down the failure modes that sabotaged this implementation in testing, and provides the production-grade architectural patterns required to guarantee fault isolation.

The simulation architecture was designed to reproduce Celery worker process termination within an asynchronous event loop. The design intended to enforce strict type validation on task payloads via Pydantic v2 and ultra-low-latency state dumping via orjson.

The intended execution lifecycle proceeds as follows:

Below is the Python implementation designed to model the worker lifecycle, signal capture, task cancellation, and resource reclamation:

💡 For immediate deployment: The complete source code suite (ZIP) for this architecture is available on Gumroad for $0+ (Pay What You Want).

During test execution across automated build verification stages and containerized test runners, this implementation failed to transition past initialization. Three distinct structural vulnerabilities caused catastrophic failures:

The script contained docstrings hinting at installation ($ pip install orjson pydantic), but the application runtime initialization was fully decoupled from the environment provisioning layer.

When invoked inside a vanilla scratch image or an unprovisioned test runner, module loading exploded at parse-time before a single instruction in main() could execute:

Because third-party binary C-extensions (orjson) and complex validation frameworks (pydantic) were imported at the module root, the failure mode was unconditional. Any downstream testing tooling relying on simple introspections or CLI dry-runs died immediately.

A well-intentioned decision was made to omit fallback implementations (e.g., standard json and dataclasses) in the name of strict coding standards and zero-cost performance optimizations.

While valid for monolithic containerized production pods, this created an anti-pattern for lightweight verification routines and CLI tools. Stripping runtime degradation mechanisms eliminated operational portability. In air-gapped test subnets, bootstrapping containers, or edge runners lacking pre-compiled C-extension wheels, the script became completely unusable.

The code attempted platform segregation inside main():

However, this guard was fundamentally flawed:

Diagnosing the failure modes of this Celery graceful shutdown prototype reveals critical guidelines for building resilient distributed worker services:

Operational runbooks stating "run pip install" fail in automated pipelines. Dependency trees must be strictly enforced via declarative configurations (pyproject.toml, lockfiles, or multi-stage Docker builds). For standalone worker testing scripts, implement graceful dynamic imports that degrade to Python's standard library (json, dataclasses, and urllib) when specialized performance libraries are unavailable.

Signal handling interacts directly with the host kernel. When designing code that must execute in heterogeneous environments (e.g., developers on Windows/macOS vs. Linux containers in Kubernetes), direct calls to loop.add_signal_handler() must be encapsulated behind an abstraction adapter or unified signal dispatcher pattern.

Premature optimization that couples code directly to high-throughput C-extensions (orjson) at the expense of environment portability introduces unneeded brittleness. High-throughput data pipelines require orjson, but bootstrapping routines, monitoring hooks, and shutdown orchestrators should always possess self-healing fallback paths to guarantee system observability under hostile runtime conditions.

If this engineering log saved your production server (and your sanity), consider supporting our architecture on GitHub Sponsors.

Codex MCP: add, configure and debug MCP servers in Codex CLI

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 5 • Originally published at vibeide.dev

Codex can call tools from Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers: documentation lookups, browsers, design files, issue trackers and more. You add a server once, and the Codex CLI, the Codex app and the IDE extension all pick it up because they share one configuration. This guide shows the exact codex mcp commands and config.toml keys from the official Codex MCP documentation and the open source Codex CLI, checked in November 2026.

Quick answer: run codex mcp add -- for a local (stdio) server or codex mcp add --url for a remote (Streamable HTTP) server, then start Codex and type /mcp to see the connected servers. Every server is stored as an [mcp_servers.] table in ~/.codex/config.toml, or in .codex/config.toml for one trusted project.

MCP is an open protocol that lets Codex use tools and context from outside programs. Codex supports two kinds of servers:

Both kinds end up in the same list, and Codex can call their tools while it works on your task.

Use codex mcp add. The usage line in the Codex source is codex mcp add [OPTIONS] (--url | -- ...): give the server a name, then either a command after -- or a URL, never both.

A local stdio server, here the Context7 documentation server used as the example in the Codex docs:

Pass environment variables to a stdio server with --env KEY=VALUE. You can repeat the flag, and it only works for stdio servers:

A remote Streamable HTTP server, with the token read from an environment variable instead of stored in the file:

Then start Codex in your project and type /mcp to check that the server connected and see what it offers.

These are the codex mcp subcommands as defined in the Codex CLI source in November 2026:

codex mcp list --json is handy in scripts, for example to check in CI that a required server is configured before you run codex exec. Our codex exec guide covers non-interactive runs.

Every server lives in a [mcp_servers.] table. You can edit the file directly instead of using codex mcp add. The stdio example from the docs:

env sets variables for the server process, and env_vars forwards variables that already exist in your shell by name. The Streamable HTTP example:

The keys you will use most, as listed in the docs in November 2026:

Keep secrets out of the file where you can. bearer_token_env_var, env_vars and env_http_headers all read values from your environment, so the config can be committed without the token.

Codex reads MCP servers from two places. ~/.codex/config.toml is the default and applies to every project. A .codex/config.toml file inside a repository adds servers for that project only, and the docs state that it works only in trusted projects.

A good split: personal tools such as a documentation server go in the global file, and tools the whole team needs for one repository (a browser for end-to-end tests, the project's issue tracker) go in the project file, committed next to your AGENTS.md. Our guide to AGENTS.md for Codex covers the instruction side of the same setup.

Many remote servers use OAuth instead of a static token. Run codex mcp login and finish the sign-in in your browser. On a machine without a browser, --no-browser prints the authorization URL and lets you paste the callback URL back. --scopes read,write overrides the scopes for one login.

If the server needs a pre-registered OAuth client, add it with the client id:

The docs say the default callback is http://127.0.0.1/callback, and that the top-level mcp_oauth_callback_port and mcp_oauth_callback_url settings fix the port or URL when a server only accepts a specific redirect. codex mcp logout removes the stored credentials.

A server can expose far more tools than you want an agent to call. Two keys narrow the list: enabled_tools is an allow list, and disabled_tools is a deny list that the docs say is applied after enabled_tools.

For approvals, the docs list default_tools_approval_mode per server (values auto, prompt, writes or approve) and a per-tool override under tools..approval_mode. tools..output_token_limit caps how much one tool's output can add to the context. MCP tools sit next to Codex's own sandbox and approval settings, which our Codex sandbox guide explains.

A useful first check for any stdio server is to run its command and args by hand in the same shell. If it fails there, it will fail inside Codex too.

Older guides show Codex started as an MCP server so another agent can call it, with tools named codex and codex-reply. OpenAI's own cookbook now marks that Codex MCP server as deprecated and kept for existing integrations, so for new automation look at codex exec or the Codex SDK instead.

If you also use Claude Code, you will configure many of the same servers there. Our Claude Code MCP guide covers its claude mcp add commands and scopes, and Codex vs Claude Code compares the two CLIs. VibeiDE is a desktop app that coordinates the Codex, Claude Code and OpenCode CLIs you already installed, so each CLI keeps its own MCP configuration; Codex runs in an embedded terminal with its original interface. Each project has its own task queue, and finished tasks wait in Ready for review. See the Codex page or start a 14-day trial, without a card, from the download page.

Run codex mcp add -- for a local server or codex mcp add --url for a remote one. Then type /mcp inside Codex to confirm it connected.

In ~/.codex/config.toml, under [mcp_servers.] tables. A project can add servers in .codex/config.toml, which Codex reads only for trusted projects.

Yes. Codex supports Streamable HTTP servers through the url key, with a bearer token from bearer_token_env_var, custom headers or OAuth via codex mcp login.

Set enabled = false in that server's table. To hide only some tools, use enabled_tools or disabled_tools.

Yes. The docs state that the desktop app, the CLI and the IDE extension share the same configuration.

Moonshot Just Put a 2.8-Trillion-Parameter Model on Hugging Face — Kimi K3.1 Pro's Open Weights Are Real, and the Fine Print Matters

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 5 • Originally published at convertelementor.com

Originally published on ConvertElementor: https://convertelementor.com/blog/moonshot-just-put-a-2-8-trillion-parameter-model-on-hugging-face-kimi-k3-1-pro-s-open-weights-are-real-and-the-fine-print-matters-november-2026-20261105

Moonshot AI published the full weights of Kimi K3.1 Pro on Hugging Face on November 4, 2026 — 2.8 trillion parameters, a 1-million-token context window, and a license that is genuinely open for most of us but not for everyone.

Moonshot posted the weights on Hugging Face under the repository name moonshotai/Kimi-K3.1-Pro — no access gate, no form, no waitlist. The download is 96 safetensors shards totalling about 1.56 terabytes.

Kimi K3.1 Pro itself is not brand new. Moonshot announced it on October 23, 2026, as the first model in the K3.1 family. What is new is that the full weights are now public.

The headline figure is 2.8 trillion total parameters, which makes K3.1 Pro the largest publicly downloadable model. It is a mixture-of-experts model: it has 896 experts, and only 16 of them activate for any given token, so 104 billion parameters are active at a time.

The context window is 1 million tokens — 1,048,576, to be exact. In 24 of the model's 93 layers, the usual attention mechanism is replaced by a different design, which is part of how it handles that long context.

"Open weights" and "open source" are not the same thing. The weights ship under Moonshot's own Kimi K3.1-Pro License, not a standard open-source license like Apache 2.0. For independent developers, small teams, and most agencies, that licence is permissive enough to use and build on. Read it before you build a product on it, especially at larger scale.

Moonshot's own numbers put K3.1 Pro near the top of the field. On Terminal-Bench 4.0, a coding-agent benchmark, it performed strongly. As with every vendor benchmark table, treat those numbers as a starting point, not proof — they measure what an agent can do, which is the result that matters most for developers.

The download button is the easy part. The weights are about 1.56 TB, and running them locally needs serious GPU hardware. The hosted price is genuinely competitive: $2 per million input tokens and $10 per million output tokens.

The single most useful thing about a model like K3.1 Pro for a WordPress developer is the combination of long context and strong coding ability. A 1-million-token context means a long-context code audit, or a debugging session across a whole theme or plugin codebase, is realistic in one conversation.

The open weights matter for a second reason specific to WordPress: sites that handle client data can, in principle, run capable models on infrastructure they control, rather than sending everything to a hosted API.

First, do not download 1.56 terabytes on a whim. Try it hosted first — on Kimi.ai, through the API, or through a hosted provider.

Second, if you run a WordPress agency, look at the long-context coding angle before anything else. Try it on a real task: a long-context code audit, or a debugging session across a whole theme.

Third, read the license before you build a product on it. For personal use, small teams, and most agencies, the terms are workable — but check them yourself.

Kimi K3.1 Pro's weights release is the rare AI story this week where the headline and the reality line up — with fine print worth reading.

Grimes to Star in the First-Ever AI-Generated Film Coming to Theaters: Watch a Clip

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The star voices a character in Gods Don't Give Gifts, but the visuals were fully generated by artificial intelligence.

Grimes has been on the cutting edge of the overlap between artificial intelligence and music, and now, she’s doing the same with the technology and film.

As revealed Thursday (Nov. 5), the alternative singer-songwriter is part of the cast of Gods Don’t Give Gifts, the first AI-generated movie coming to theaters. She lends her voice and physical likeness to her character, Zayinth Alda, described in a release as “a disaffected NiiroCore aristocrat and niece to Caen Halvorith, inventor of the Substrate Migration Protocol.”

The film, which will premiere this winter, explores a dystopian future reality in which “consciousness can be transferred between bodies, memory can be bought and sold, and death is no longer final,” a synopsis reads. In a clip released alongside Grimes’ casting announcement, her AI-animated form chastises another character before storming away with a massive, muscular, faceless creature acting as her guard.

“You dragged me to the slag heaps for this? A slideshow of derivative drivel?” she yells before boarding her futuristic spacecraft.

Though the visuals on screen are produced by AI, Grimes’ voice and dialogue were not. Gossip Goblin, the studio behind the Zack London-directed project, asserts in the release, “Casting the likeness of an actor with their spoken vocal performance will become more common with animated films using AI — audiences will see actors, athletes, musicians giving vocal performances with their likeness in films.”

The announcement is fitting, considering Grimes has long been interested in the ways the controversial technology is developing. She’s now gearing up to release her new album, Psy Opera, which explores the complexities of AI and uses artificially generated music on one of the tracks.

Watch the clip of Grimes’ AI-assisted performance in Gods Don’t Give Gifts below.

This Coffee Subscription Delivers Some of the Freshest Beans We’ve Tried

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It's one of the best gifts you can give to coffee lovers this holiday season.

Our editors and experts handpick every product we feature. We may earn a commission from your purchases.Learn more.

It really doesn't get fresher than this. A Storyville coffee subscription delivers delicious beans right to your doorstep.

We’ve all been there. Standing in the grocery store staring at dozens of coffee brands and trying to decide which one might perk us up, only to end up scowling at a subpar cup of sludge the next morning. Well, those days are over because Storyville, one of Seattle’s best coffee shops, now delivers freshly roasted beans straight to your door.

I tried a Storyville coffee subscription, and I assure you that you’ll never have to waste your time (or money) on stale beans again. Here’s everything you need to know about this caffeinated club.

Storyville is one of the highest-rated coffee shops in Seattle, with locations at Pike Place and at 1st and Madison. They also have a roasting studio you can tour to see exactly where your coffee beans are roasted before making their way to your door. But if you can’t make it to Seattle, they also offer coffee subscription services with nationwide shipping.

The brand sources premium-grade green coffees and roasts each bean varietal individually before blending. Beans are roasted on the darker end of medium and feature a rich, natural sweetness with notes of chocolate and caramel. Unlike many other dark roasts, this coffee has a smooth finish and no bitterness. Best of all, Storyville ships its coffee immediately after roasting.

Storyville subscriptions are highly customizable in order to fit your needs. First, choose between whole beans and single-serve pods. Then choose the quantity and delivery frequency that best suits your needs. You can pause, skip or cancel via text at any time. Moreover, you’ll receive 20% off, among other perks.

I am a coffee fiend. On any given day, I’m three cups in by 9 a.m. That’s why I couldn’t wait to get my hands on a bag of real Seattle coffee.

My order arrived on Wednesday, October 28. I received a bag of Prologue coffee beans, Sunrise espresso beans and Epilogue decaf coffee beans. I knew the roast dates would be stamped on the bags, but I was amazed to see that my beans had been roasted just days prior, on October 25 and October 27. Talk about fresh!

I opened the Prologue first and poured the entire bag into the bean hopper of my De’Longhi Rivelia. After brewing, I took a sip before adding my cream and sugar and was amazed by how smooth it was. It was fresh and flavorful without a hint of bitterness. I love a dark roast and could drink this all day long.

The next day, I opened the Sunrise espresso beans. I used it to make a regular cup of coffee followed by an Americano. Like the Prologue, the beans were clearly fresh, and the coffee was exceptionally smooth! I’ll be saving this bag along with the decaf for espresso martinis with my girlfriends.

As a dark roast gal, I’ve had my fair share of coffee that tastes bitter or burnt. I didn’t get any notes of bitterness from Storyville coffee. I’ll also point out that a bag of Prologue coffee lasted one week. Given how much coffee I drink plus the fact that my husband tends to make a cup here and there, I’m not surprised we went through it so quickly. Luckily, the subscription is completely customizable. If you drink as much coffee as I do, you’ll want to order two or three bags at a time. But if you just enjoy one cup per day, then one bag at a time will suffice.

That said, these beans are so fresh we wouldn’t be surprised if you start drinking more coffee than usual. And we certainly wouldn’t blame you!

It’s also easy to send a subscription as a gift. Simply head over to their gifting page and opt for three, five, eight or 12 prepaid shipments.

GM Patents Launch Control That Watches the Drag Strip Christmas Tree, Launches for You

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The recent patent describes a system that manages the brakes and throttle to deliver remarkably consistent drag strip blasts away from the line.

Getting a great launch at the drag strip takes more than horsepower and sticky tires. Drivers also need to time their start perfectly; even a tenth of a second can make a difference. Now, General Motors has patented technology that could take that last bit of human involvement out of the equation entirely.

The system uses the vehicle's cameras to watch the drag strip's Christmas Tree, predict when the light will turn green, and launch the car automatically. Better yet, it can actually begin preparing the launch before the green light illuminates. We might be about to see the age of push-button drag racing.

The technology is detailed in U.S. Patent 12,780,203, titled “Methods and Systems for Coordinating Launch Control for Vehicles for Racing.” Originally filed in January 2025, the application was published in July 2026 before finally receiving its patent grant on November 3.

At its core, the system builds on the launch control technology already available in modern performance cars. Right now, most launch control systems work very similarly: the driver applies full brakes and full throttle, and holds both pedals until it's time to go; they let off the brake, and the car rockets away. GM's proposed technology takes over most of that process.

The craziest bit is just how it manages all of this. First, it uses on-board cameras to identify the Christmas tree at the drag strip. Once it recognizes the lights, it notifies the driver and prompts them to activate launch control. Those who want to try this new method could control it much the same as they do with existing systems; the patent describes drivers putting their feet on both pedals and then, once the automatic launch system takes over, the driver can remove both feet and let the car work.

The car can maintain braking pressure and staging torque automatically, even after the driver releases the pedals. The clever part comes next: rather than simply waiting for the green light, the software identifies the sequence of yellow lights and compares it against stored timing patterns to calculate precisely when the race will begin.

That's key, because different events run the lights at different times. For example, a Pro Tree illuminates all three amber lights simultaneously before turning green 0.4 seconds later, whereas Sportsman Tree illuminates the amber lights sequentially with half-second intervals. GM's system could account for those differences automatically. The system is even capable of calculating the delay between commanding movement from the vehicle and the vehicle actually moving forward.

That all said, this is just a patent for now. GM doesn't mention any specific vehicle or plan for production in its filing. If it does bring it to market, we'd be eager to see how close it can come to cutting a perfect light.

From discovering new opportunities to promote Zanda and negotiating great deals to coordinating the deliverables and measuring their impact, you'll play an important part in how we grow. Manage campaign operations, including advertising calendars, budgets, pipelines, and vendor coordination.

Track and evaluate performance, using campaign data and insights to identify what's working, recommend improvements, and maximize the value of our advertising investments.

Familiarity with Paid Social, Display, Paid Search, or emerging advertising channels.

Experience with tools such as HubSpot, Google Analytics, Canva, Adobe Creative Suite, or project management platforms.

An understanding of marketing metrics such as CAC, CPA, ROAS, attribution, or UTM tracking.

Experience in SaaS, HealthTech, or using AI and automation tools to improve marketing workflows.

A natural relationship builder. You enjoy connecting with people, creating genuine partnerships, and aren't afraid to negotiate or ask for a better deal.

Everything Announced at GOG’s First-Ever Showcase Event

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Covering the video games industry since 2017, with experience in news, articles, and reviews. Currently a writer at DualShockers and gaming magazines.

I know that I'm not the only one who has always appreciated GOG as a sacred sanctuary for classic PC titles, but its latest event proves the platform is doing much more than just keeping old games running.

That's because, to celebrate its 18th anniversary, the team held its first-ever showcase, and it was packed with updates that have me genuinely excited about where the platform is heading – and you should be too!

Broadcast today, Thursday, November 5, on YouTube, the first-ever GOG Showcase caught everyone's attention with a variety of announcements, ranging from new games and features to an entirely new publishing initiative.

For starters, co-founder Miho Kinski perfectly summed up the platform's philosophy at the beginning of the event, making it clear that its mission remains unchanged: "We wanted games to live forever. Not for a season, not until the servers shut down, but for as long as people want them to play." And honestly, that message really resonated throughout the presentation.

Then, the event kicked off by celebrating the GOG Patrons program, and the numbers are pretty impressive: more than 10,000 supporters have helped preserve over 300 games, ensuring they run flawlessly on modern systems with the help of more than 1,700 technical fixes.

Bit Kid shadowdrops Knytt Classic for free on Steam and GOG.

And speaking of games, the showcase also had plenty of nostalgia in store, with the announcement of Wing Commander Anniversary Editions. The series is returning in grand fashion with its 35th and 30th Anniversary collections, featuring both legacy and updated versions of the games. Oh, and if you already own these titles, the new editions will automatically appear in your library!

The expansion of GOG's classic game collection doesn't stop there, either. The original Road Rash has also officially joined the platform today, giving fans another opportunity to revisit this beloved classic.

The event kicked off by celebrating the GOG Patrons program, and the numbers are pretty impressive: more than 10,000 supporters have helped preserve over 300 games, ensuring they run flawlessly on modern systems with the help of more than 1,700 technical fixes.

However, perhaps the biggest surprise was the reveal of Retrovibe by GOG, a new publishing initiative focused on bringing previously unreleased games with a "classic soul" to modern audiences. This marks the company's first major step into publishing, and it's an interesting evolution for a store that has spent nearly two decades acting as a digital harbor for gaming history.

Fans can look forward to bringing lost games back from the dead.

And when it comes to new features, that wasn't all the showcase had to offer. GOG also introduced GOG Social, a new social network integrated into the platform that allows users to share screenshots, videos, and participate in discussions, all directly connected to the games they own.

Finally, the team confirmed what many of us have been asking for: a completely rebuilt GOG GALAXY. The launcher has been redesigned for Windows and Mac, promising better performance and, most importantly, full Linux support. Yeah... I used to pray for times like this!

Eighteen years of keeping games alive is no small feat, and based on these announcements, the next chapter for GOG looks just as bright. As the team emphasized during its first-ever showcase, "18 years of making games live forever together." I've always relied on the platform to revisit some of my favorite childhood titles, but with these new social features and its expansion into publishing, I have a feeling I'll be spending a lot more time exploring its library in the years to come!

The Video Game History Foundation just tripled its public research library!

Russell Brand Trial Reveals Worst & Best Of TV Industry That Protected, Then Exposed A Predator

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Russell Brand has been convicted of rape and sexual assault after spending the best part of a month on trial. In many ways, the business that propelled Brand to fame and power was also in the dock.

In the first major #MeToo court case for the British television industry, London’s Southwark Crown Court heard from multiple complainants and witnesses about a culture that empowered Brand’s voracious sexual appetite — and did little to stop him crossing boundaries of consent.

As his former assistant put it in court: “I have worked with celebrities before and have seen a lot of people normalizing bad behavior, but this was on another level.” Related Stories News Russell Brand's Lawyer Rounds On Journalist Who Exposed Brand's Behavior News Russell Brand Found Guilty Of Rape & Sexual Assault

Four of the six women who made police complaints about Brand worked in the television and radio industry. Three of those four convinced a jury that they were the survivors of brazen sexual assault when Brand was at the peak of his powers. Watch on Deadline

Brand was convicted of grabbing the breasts of an ex-MTV employee at an industry party in 2004. He was found guilty of pushing a woman against a wall and molesting her at the offices of a major radio company in 2004 or 2005. Brand was also convicted of burying his head in the genitals of a former Channel 4 employee during the recording of a DVD in 2009.

In each case, the consequences for Brand were not obvious, despite the women raising concerns about his behaviour shortly after they were assaulted.

The ex-MTV employee was introduced to Brand by a well-known TV producer who was hosting the party at London’s Nordic Bar. In Brand’s words, he was told by the producer, “There’s a bird here who wants to meet you.” Within minutes, Brand had sexually assaulted her. It’s no wonder the prosecution argued that Brand treated women in the industry as a “disposable commodity.”

The ex-MTV employee raised concerns with the producer after the party and the court heard how he confronted Brand’s associates and told them the behavior was “not acceptable.” It was not enough to stall Brand’s rising star. Asked by Brand’s defense why she didn’t initially report the allegation to the police, the woman said she feared being “seen as trouble” and “the media industry protects its own.”

The former Channel 4 employee was sexually assaulted in front of two producers, one of whom continues to work on prominent British shows. In evidence, she said the producers shrugged as Brand demanded a massage and put his head between her legs, before laughing. She reported the incident to her Channel 4 line manager at the time, but it’s taken 17 years for her complaint to be fully heard.

In a statement after Brand’s conviction, a Channel 4 spokesperson said: “Our thoughts are first and foremost with the six women involved in the case against Russell Brand, including those affected by the crimes for which he has been convicted. We recognise the profound impact of these crimes and commend the extraordinary courage of all those who gave evidence as prosecution witnesses at trial.”

While Channel 4 did not do enough to protect its former employee in 2009, the British broadcaster can legitimately claim credit for eventually bringing Brand to justice. The network partnered with The Times and The Sunday Times on an investigation into Brand’s conduct, resulting in the 2023 documentary Russell Brand: In Plain Sight.

The film was referenced repeatedly during the Southwark Crown Court trial. It did not feature any of the women at the heart of criminal proceedings but was the catalyst for their police complaints. It was the reason why, as the prosecution explained, the women “realized they are not alone and have bravely come before you to tell the truth.”

Louisa Compton, Channel 4’s director of news, digital and audience trust, put it like this: “The outcome of the Russell Brand trial belongs to all of the women who had the courage to speak out … The trial heard that women involved in the criminal case came forward after seeing the Channel 4 Dispatches film. It is a powerful demonstration of why fearless, independent, investigative journalism in the public interest matters.”

Russell Brand may have been on trial, but the court proceedings revealed the worst and best of the British TV industry.

2028 QB Tristin Gaines sets commitment date, Oregon among finalists

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Not long ago, the Oregon Ducks were well-positioned to land one of the top quarterbacks in the 2028 recruiting cycle as their first commitment in the class. Now, their chances are less likely, but a top target is set to come off the board very soon.

2028 quarterback Tristin Gaines, the No. 9 quarterback and No. 85 player overall in the class, is set to announce his commitment on Nov. 14, with his group of finalists including Oregon, SMU, Houston, LSU and Florida. He will announce his decision live on the Rivals YouTube channel.

The Ducks have been closely linked to Gaines for some time, and even started to grow closer to him than fellow quarterback recruit Josiah Boyd, who ultimately committed to UCLA. Gaines has been to Eugene for games twice this fall after picking up an offer in the summer, and it looked like just a matter of time before the Texas native landed in Oregon's class.

However, as things often do in recruiting, the race has taken a turn. SMU, almost four hours north of Gaines' home in Waller, has been "setting the pace" in the recruitment, according to Rivals' Sam Spiegelman, and is considered the favorite right now. Gaines will visit LSU this weekend, for the second time in a month, as the Tigers host Alabama.

Gaines is a dual-threat under center, passing for 2,016 yards and 22 touchdowns as a sophomore while rushing for 542 yards and nine more scores. In nine games this season, Gaines has completed 56% of his passes for 2,223 yards, 24 touchdowns and three interceptions while rushing for 580 yards and two scores. Gaines is also a star on the baseball diamond, winning a pair of gold medals with USA Baseball.

Oregon has continued to make Gaines a priority in their class, but the Ducks appear to have lost some steam in recent weeks, and the team's disappointing season and firing of offensive coordinator Drew Mehringer could have some impact. Quarterbacks coach Koa Ka'ai has been leading the recruitment for Gaines and even went to visit him last month.

The Ducks are still searching for their first commitment in the 2028 class. Oregon has some ground to make up, but no matter what direction the commitment goes, Gaines will likely still be recruited by Oregon heading into the offseason.

White House Twitter account faceplants with awful Zelda AI slop that missed the midterms by two days

Donald Trump’s staff apparently loves games, especially that one where we play as that boy Zelda, so they released a self-endorsement Ocarina of Time-inspired AI slop video to accompany the release of the game’s eagerly anticipated remaster.

It's dangerous to go alone! Take this 🇺🇸 pic.twitter.com/masCWWQe7m

It’s embarrassing for many reasons. First off, it’s impossible to ignore how this is one of the first official statements coming out of the White House after the Republican Party’s heavy defeat in the midterm elections.

Then, there’s how the iconic red hat that once bravely displayed the infamous message about making America great again has been replaced by one that says “Still your President”. For the first time in a while, it feels less like a threat, more like a desperate reminder.

It gives the ick—a relatively new expression used to describe the moment when all the attraction you feel for a prospective love interest dies immediately because of something random they do. People in politics have been talking for a while about simple actions that immediately killed a candidate’s credibility: when Nixon supposedly lost the election to Kennedy because he was sweating too profusely during a debate, when Howard Dean supposedly lost his bid for the White House because he let out a silly-sounding scream during a celebration, or when Hillary Clinton told us all to Pokémon Go to the polls. I don’t know about all the previous ones, but I remember feeling right there and then that Hillary was toast.

And I just can’t help but feel like Trump pulling this after such a massive defeat feels like his own “Pokémon Go to the polls” moment, or, should I say, his “Ocarina of Time” to go to the polls. I know it’s actually too late for that, but doesn’t that make it even more on-brand?

The FAA is finally phasing out floppy disks from air traffic control

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The US Federal Aviation Administration is getting some probably overdue upgrades. The flight agency announced today that it is modernizing some air traffic control systems that have to date been running on floppy disks. You read that right: floppy disks.

For those of you too young to remember, floppy disks were precursors to CDs as a means of storing and running digital data, particularly computer files and programs. IBM developed the first one back in 1971. The FAA systems in question date back to the 1980s. Unsurprisingly, the bulletin from the agency says the coming upgrades will give air traffic controllers faster access to information, reduce repair expenses and improve safety for public air travel.

The FAA's diskette systems contain "vital weather and other critical information" for air traffic controllers, and they're still in use at more than 400 facilities around the country. They'll be replaced by a more modern system called Enterprise – Information Display System (E-IDS), which is comprised of a digital display for relaying status information, updates and other alerts to air traffic teams. The FAA plans to have E-IDS installed in 435 sites by the end of 2028; the first five locations to receive the upgrades are the Atlantic City International Airport, Nashville International Airport and Greater Rochester International Airport, as well as data processing hubs in Atlanta and Salt Lake City.

Despite the age of floppy disk tech, they've actually remained more common in government systems than you might expect. The US Air Force only just ended its use of 8-inch diskettes tied to nuclear missile launch silos in 2019. The Japanese government announced in 2024 that it had phased out all but one of its systems that still relied on floppy disks.

10 workouts and conditions tied to exertional rhabdo, from spin to military

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Exertional rhabdomyolysis workouts range from spin classes to military training; here are 10 workouts and conditions tied to the muscle injury and its warning signs

Exercise is supposed to break muscle down a little so it can rebuild stronger. Push the dose far enough past what the body is ready for, and the damage stops being a training signal. It becomes a medical problem that can reach the kidneys.

That condition is exertional rhabdomyolysis, often shortened to rhabdo. It is the breakdown of skeletal muscle fibers brought on by physical effort. When those fibers rupture, their contents spill into the bloodstream. Two of those contents matter most. Myoglobin is a protein that stores oxygen inside muscle cells. A heavy load of it in the blood can injure the kidneys. Creatine kinase, or CK, is an enzyme that doctors use to gauge how much muscle has been damaged.

The classic warning signs are severe muscle pain, weakness and dark urine the color of tea. Many patients do not show all three at once. Swelling in the affected muscles can also appear, along with soreness far beyond the usual post-workout ache.

Diagnosis rests on a blood test for CK. A level of roughly five times the upper limit of normal is a commonly used threshold for the condition. Treatment is medical care, centered mainly on intravenous fluids.

The risk does not come from any single sport. It builds from a familiar set of contributors: unaccustomed or extreme exertion, heavy eccentric work in which muscles lengthen under load, heat and dehydration. Sickle cell trait, certain drugs and supplements, and a recent illness can also raise the odds. Those factors show up across very different settings, from a boutique fitness studio to a military training base.

The slides that follow cover exertional rhabdomyolysis workouts and conditions — 10 settings where those factors can converge. Some are named formats, such as indoor cycling classes, CrossFit-style sessions and college football conditioning. Others are circumstances that can turn an ordinary routine into a risky one. Examples include a hard return after a long layoff and training in hot, humid weather. The list is a selection, not a ranking. It does not claim to cover every way the injury occurs, and its order says nothing about which activity causes the most cases.

Case reports in the medical literature describe exertional rhabdomyolysis after spin classes, mostly in beginners who rode hard in one of their first sessions. The common thread is an unconditioned rider working against heavy resistance at high effort.

Indoor cycling classes use stationary bikes with a weighted flywheel. Each rider controls the resistance with a knob or lever. An instructor sets the pace, often to music, and calls out climbs, sprints and standing intervals. That format lets a newcomer try to match regulars who have trained for months, even when the newcomer's legs are not ready for the load.

The thighs do most of the work on a spin bike, especially the quadriceps at the front of the leg. In published spin-related cases, thigh pain and swelling are the dominant complaints. That fits the mechanics of the exercise. Every pedal stroke loads the same muscle group, and a hard class strings together long efforts with little recovery between them.

The case reports do not establish how often this happens. No reliable incidence figure exists for spin classes. What the reports share is the profile of the rider. They mostly involve people new to the format rather than experienced cyclists who have built up tolerance over time.

The warning signs are easy to misread after a first class. Some soreness after any new workout is expected, so a rider may dismiss thigh pain that is far worse than usual. Pain severe enough to make walking or climbing stairs difficult is a reason to seek medical care. So are legs that swell or feel weak, or urine that turns dark after the ride.

For first-time riders, the risk described in these reports comes from the gap between conditioning and effort. Resistance is the variable a rider controls most directly. Keeping it lower in early classes and raising it over several sessions narrows that gap.

CrossFit-style workouts that pack very high repetition counts into a short window are a recognized setting for exertional rhabdomyolysis. CrossFit has acknowledged the risk through its own cartoon mascot, Uncle Rhabdo.

Uncle Rhabdo is drawn as a clown hooked up to a dialysis machine, with his kidneys and other organs spilling onto the floor. He came from CrossFit itself, not from outside critics. The image links the training style's intensity to the kidney damage that severe muscle breakdown can cause. A second clown, Pukie, represents the vomiting that can follow a hard session.

The danger in this format comes from density. A single session can call for dozens of repetitions of one movement, such as pull-ups, push-ups, burpees or wall-ball shots. Many workouts are scored on time. Others ask for as many rounds as possible, a format known as AMRAP, within a fixed window. Rest between rounds tends to be short or absent. Muscles pushed toward failure again and again in a compressed period accumulate damage faster than they can recover.

Newcomers are a particular concern. A person new to this style of training may try to match the pace or volume of experienced members in a group class. Their muscles have not adapted to that workload. Going all out from the first session skips the gradual build-up that conditioning normally provides.

CrossFit programming includes scaling, which means cutting the load, the repetitions or the difficulty of a movement to fit an athlete's current ability. Scaling lets a beginner follow the same workout structure at a lower dose. It is the format's own built-in tool for limiting how much new work a muscle takes on at once.

Published medical case reports have described rhabdo after CrossFit-style training. Those reports do not establish how often it happens. They do show the condition turning up in ordinary gyms, not only on military bases or at endurance events.

Early symptoms after a high-rep session can pass for normal soreness. The difference is degree. Pain, swelling or weakness far out of proportion to the workout calls for a doctor, not another training day. After a session heavy in pull-ups or push-ups, those symptoms may concentrate in the upper arms.

Other factors can compound the strain of a dense, high-volume workout. Dehydration, a recent illness and certain drugs or supplements are all recognized contributors.

Military training is a setting where exertional rhabdomyolysis is formally tracked. The U.S. Armed Forces Health Surveillance Division reports on cases among active-component service members in its Medical Surveillance Monthly Report, known as the MSMR.

The division draws those counts from the Defense Medical Surveillance System, a database of medical encounters across the services.

That surveillance shows the injury is not spread evenly across the force. The reports break cases down by service branch, sex, age and race. Rates differ across each of those groups.

Three conditions recur as drivers in military settings: heat, an abrupt ramp-up in training and repeated exertion without enough rest. Each one adds strain to muscle in a different way. Recruit training can combine all three in the same week.

Much military conditioning happens outdoors, often in uniform and gear. Heat raises the physical load of any given effort. When it is paired with dehydration, working muscle is under more stress than the same workout would put on it in cool conditions.

Recruits arrive from civilian life with a wide range of fitness levels. Basic training then puts them on a fixed schedule of daily physical training, marching and drills. For someone who was mostly inactive beforehand, that change in workload is abrupt. Muscle that has not adapted to a workload is more vulnerable to breakdown when it suddenly meets one.

The third driver is repetition across days. Damaged muscle fibers need time to recover. When a unit trains hard again before that recovery happens, damage from one session can add to damage from the next. A volume of work that a body tolerates once can become a problem when it is repeated without rest.

Recruits also have limited control over the pace. Training follows a unit schedule, so a trainee cannot simply skip a session to let sore muscles recover.

In basic training, soreness is part of the routine, which can make warning signs easier to dismiss. Pain far beyond what the day's training should cause is one warning sign. Weakness that makes ordinary tasks hard, such as climbing stairs or lifting a rifle, is another. Swelling or dark urine is also a reason to see medical staff.

In January 2011, 13 University of Iowa football players were hospitalized with rhabdomyolysis after a single offseason workout built around back squats. The episode is a well-documented example of exertional rhabdo striking several athletes on the same team at once.

The players involved were not beginners. They were Division I athletes on a Big Ten roster. Fitness built over months of training does not fully protect against a session that demands far more volume than the body has recently handled.

The back squat helps explain the scale of the injury. The lift loads the largest muscles in the body — the quadriceps, hamstrings and glutes — along with the lower back. When a workout piles many sets of a lift like that into one session, a large mass of muscle tissue absorbs damage at the same time. More damaged tissue means more myoglobin and creatine kinase spilling into the bloodstream.

Timing was the other factor. The workout came during the offseason, when players are not in the weekly rhythm of games and in-season practice. A sudden jump in volume after time away from heavy training is one of the clearest patterns behind exertional rhabdo, whatever an athlete's baseline fitness.

A team setting widens the reach of that risk. Everyone in a group session does the same work, at the same time, under the same coaching plan. A session that overwhelms one player can overwhelm many.

Warning signs can be easy to dismiss in football. Sore, heavy legs are routine after a squat day. The difference is degree: pain and swelling well beyond normal soreness, weakness that makes walking or climbing stairs difficult, or urine that turns dark. Those signs call for prompt medical evaluation rather than another day of training.

The Iowa hospitalizations show how a standard lift can become dangerous through dosage. The lesson concerns the size of the first heavy session after a break, not the choice of exercise.

Heavy eccentric training, in which a muscle lengthens while resisting a load, damages muscle fibers more than raising the same weight does. Slow negatives and high-volume lowering work are the clearest examples. They are a recognized route to exertional rhabdomyolysis when the dose outruns a lifter's conditioning.

Every repetition has two halves. The concentric phase shortens the muscle, as when the arms curl a dumbbell up. The eccentric phase lengthens it under tension, as when the dumbbell is lowered back down with control. Lowering into a squat, descending from a pull-up bar and running downhill all load muscles eccentrically.

Muscles can produce more force while lengthening than while shortening. That lets a lifter control a weight on the way down that they could not lift on the way up. Some programs use this through negatives, in which a partner helps raise a heavy load and the lifter lowers it alone. Other programs stretch the lowering phase to several seconds per repetition.

Eccentric contractions also cost less energy than concentric ones at the same load. A set can feel manageable, with less breathlessness and burn, while the muscle absorbs substantial mechanical strain. That gap between the effort a lifter feels and the damage the muscle takes makes eccentric-heavy sessions easy to misjudge.

The strain shows up as microscopic disruption inside the muscle fibers. In most cases the result is delayed onset muscle soreness, which typically builds over the following one to three days. Exercise physiologists use eccentric protocols in laboratory research because they reliably raise markers of muscle damage, including creatine kinase.

The line between ordinary soreness and rhabdo is a matter of degree. Pain far beyond normal stiffness points to a medical problem rather than a hard workout. So does a limb that swells or loses strength, or urine that turns dark. Blood markers of muscle damage after eccentric work can keep climbing for days. Symptoms that worsen instead of easing are a reason for a medical evaluation.

Muscles adapt to this kind of loading quickly. One eccentric session substantially reduces the damage caused by a similar session later, a phenomenon known as the repeated bout effect. The risk is concentrated in the first exposures, especially when volume or load is high from the start.

Long-distance races reliably push creatine kinase above normal, yet most finishers never develop clinical exertional rhabdomyolysis. Marathons and ultramarathons serve as a contrast case. They show that a high lab number and a dangerous muscle injury are not the same thing.

A marathon covers 26.2 miles, or 42.195 kilometers. An ultramarathon is any race longer than that. Common ultra distances include 50 kilometers, 100 kilometers and 100 miles. Hours of repeated foot strikes cause small-scale damage to muscle fibers over the whole course. That damage lets CK leak out of injured muscle cells. As a result, its level climbs in the blood after a race.

A raised CK reading on its own does not settle a diagnosis. Rhabdomyolysis as a clinical condition involves muscle breakdown heavy enough to cause symptoms. In serious cases, the myoglobin released from that muscle puts the kidneys at risk. Many runners with elevated post-race levels feel nothing worse than the stiff legs that follow any long effort. Serious cases among race finishers are uncommon.

Preparation explains much of the gap. Runners who build mileage over months condition their muscles to the specific stress of distance running. The protection that develops after an initial damaging session is known as the repeated-bout effect. A well-prepared marathoner reaches race day with muscles already adapted to hours of impact. That places a planned race in a different category from a workout the body has never done before.

Risk rises at the extremes. The longest ultramarathon distances carry more exposure than a standard marathon. Hot race-day weather and dehydration also raise the odds of tipping from ordinary soreness into a medical problem. Starting a race while recovering from a recent illness adds to the strain. So does taking certain drugs or supplements that affect muscle or kidney function.

The practical warning for distance runners is pain out of proportion to the effort. Legs that stay weak instead of loosening up over the following days are one signal. Urine that turns dark brown, like cola, is another. Either is a reason to get medical care promptly rather than rest at home and wait it out.

Returning to full-intensity training after weeks or months away is one of the best-established triggers for exertional rhabdomyolysis. The core problem is unaccustomed exertion. Muscles that have lost conditioning are asked to absorb a workload they once handled but no longer can.

A layoff can start in many ways. An injury, a surgery, a demanding stretch at work, a holiday season or an offseason can all pull a trained person away from the gym or the road. The reason for the break matters less than the loss of muscle tolerance that builds while it lasts.

Exercise physiology has a name for that tolerance. The repeated bout effect describes how one session of unfamiliar exercise protects muscle against damage from a similar session later on. Regular training keeps that protection in place. Time away lets it fade. Returning muscle ends up closer to the state of muscle that has never done the exercise at all.

Experienced athletes face a specific risk here. They tend to remember the weights, distances, paces and repetition counts they handled before the break. Picking up at those numbers on the first day back can cause far more muscle damage than the identical session would have caused at peak fitness.

Illness adds another layer. A recent illness is a recognized contributor to exertional rhabdomyolysis. When sickness was the reason for the layoff, the returning athlete may carry two risk factors into the same workout.

Telling ordinary soreness apart from muscle breakdown can be hard in the first day or two after a comeback session. Stiffness that is uncomfortable but manageable is expected. Pain far out of proportion to the workout, swollen or weak muscles and dark urine point to something more serious. Those signs call for medical evaluation.

The practical response is a gradual return. Starting well below previous loads and volumes, then building across several sessions, gives muscle time to readapt. That build-up restores the protective effect the break erased before the hardest efforts resume.

Hard exercise in heat and humidity raises the risk of exertional rhabdomyolysis because the body has to cool itself and fuel working muscle at the same time. Heat and dehydration are recognized contributors to the muscle breakdown, and they add to the strain of an overly demanding workout.

Sweat is the body's main cooling tool during exercise. It works when the moisture evaporates from the skin. Humid air slows that evaporation, so sweat drips off without carrying away much heat. As a result, core temperature climbs faster, even at a pace that would feel manageable on a cool, dry day.

The heart then faces competing demands. Blood is sent to the skin to shed heat, while working muscles need blood to deliver oxygen and clear waste. As fluid is lost through sweat, total blood volume shrinks. Less blood reaches the muscles and the kidneys, and the strain on both grows.

Dehydration matters twice in rhabdo. It makes muscle fibers more vulnerable during the workout. It also leaves the kidneys less able to clear the myoglobin that damaged muscle releases afterward. Concentrated urine and reduced kidney blood flow give that protein more opportunity to cause injury.

Heat-related muscle breakdown can also overlap with exertional heatstroke. Heatstroke is a medical emergency in which core body temperature rises to dangerous levels. Rhabdomyolysis is a known complication of heatstroke. Confusion or collapse during hard exercise in heat calls for emergency care rather than rest and fluids at home.

Heat acclimatization is the body's gradual adjustment to training in warm weather. Over repeated sessions, sweating begins earlier and plasma volume expands. Coaches and military trainers use a measure called wet-bulb globe temperature to decide when to scale back outdoor work. It accounts for humidity, sun and wind as well as air temperature.

Dark urine after a session in a heat wave or a humid gym is a reason to seek medical care. So is muscle pain or swelling out of proportion to the workout. A CK blood test can confirm whether muscle breakdown is under way.

High-intensity bootcamp and interval classes can lead to exertional rhabdomyolysis when a participant stacks repeated all-out efforts with little recovery. The format packs a large volume of hard muscular work into a short window.

Bootcamp sessions typically string together bodyweight drills such as burpees, jump squats, walking lunges, push-ups and mountain climbers. Many of those movements load the same muscle groups again and again. The thighs and glutes, for example, may absorb squats, lunges and jump landings within a few minutes of each other.

Jumping drills add a specific kind of strain. Each landing forces the quadriceps to lengthen while they brake the body's fall. That lengthening action, known as an eccentric contraction, is the type most closely tied to muscle damage. A class built around jump squats, box jumps or skater hops repeats it many times in a single session.

Interval formats compress effort further. The protocol associated with Japanese researcher Izumi Tabata calls for 20 seconds of maximal work followed by 10 seconds of rest, repeated for eight rounds. Tabata's original research used stationary bikes, not the mixed bodyweight circuits that now often carry the name. Many classes borrow that structure or similar work-to-rest ratios. Short rests limit how much the muscles recover between bouts.

The group setting adds a separate pressure. Participants follow the instructor's clock rather than their own condition. Some classes also display heart rates or scores on screens around the room. Keeping pace with a room of regulars can push a participant to work harder than their muscles are prepared for.

Ordinary soreness and an injury tend to follow different patterns. Delayed onset muscle soreness usually peaks one to three days after exercise and eases with gentle movement. Pain after a bootcamp class that keeps building needs medical evaluation. So does soreness that comes with swelling, marked weakness or dark urine.

Sickle cell trait is a recognized risk factor for exertional rhabdomyolysis during hard, sustained exercise. Athletes who carry it face greater danger when a session pushes them to maximal effort without enough rest between bouts.

The trait is not the same as sickle cell disease. A person with the trait inherited one copy of the gene for sickle hemoglobin and one copy for normal hemoglobin. Most carriers have no symptoms in daily life, and many do not know they have it. Someone with sickle cell disease inherited two affected copies and lives with a chronic illness.

The risk emerges under physical stress. During intense exertion, red blood cells in a carrier can change into a rigid, curved sickle shape. Those cells can clog small blood vessels in working muscle. Muscle deprived of oxygen-rich blood can then break down quickly. Heat, dehydration and the thinner air at high altitude can add to that strain.

In a carrier, the breakdown can surface during the workout itself rather than the next morning. An athlete may slump with sudden weakness or report muscle pain well beyond normal fatigue. Dark urine afterward is another signal. Any of these calls for prompt medical care.

College sports have taken up the issue. The NCAA has made sickle cell trait part of its athlete-screening and precaution policy. That brings the condition to the attention of team medical staff. Knowing an athlete's status lets coaches and trainers plan sessions with it in mind.

The common precautions follow the same logic that applies to other rhabdo risks. Intensity builds gradually across weeks rather than days. Longer rest between hard repetitions lets muscle recover before the next bout. Stopping a drill at the first sign of unusual weakness takes priority over finishing it.

The trait does not rule anyone out of sport. Carriers compete at every level, from high school to the professional ranks. Screening gives staff a chance to account for a known vulnerability, both in how a workout is designed and in how they respond to early symptoms.

→ Baca artikel asli

OpenAI Found 948 Linux Security Flaws—And Figured Out How to Exploit Them

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

OpenAI Found 948 Linux Security Flaws—And Figured Out How to Exploit Them

OpenAI says its autonomous security agents discovered 948 distinct vulnerabilities in the Linux kernel, developed working exploits and drafted fixes for review—all for roughly $1.2 million in API credits.

“We chose Linux to study how effectively frontier models could find vulnerabilities and demonstrate their security impact in a codebase of this scale and importance,” OpenAI said in results published Wednesday.

The Linux kernel powers cloud servers, Android devices and much of the world's critical computing infrastructure. The flaws identified in OpenAI's experiment were local privilege-escalation vulnerabilities: An attacker would first need limited access to a vulnerable machine, but could then potentially obtain root privileges and take full control.

OpenAI's agents scanned about 2.8 million lines of kernel code, or roughly 7% of the project. “Our prototype identified and analyzed 2,308 candidate Linux kernel findings, dynamically reproduced 1,724 vulnerabilities, and produced 948 local privilege escalation (LPE) exploits that could allow an unprivileged local user to gain root access” OpenAI said.

After grouping exploits by their underlying cause and proposed fix, researchers counted 948 distinct flaws.

The October run cost $1,213,430 in API credits, equivalent to around $1,279 per vulnerability. OpenAI compared that with historical $20,000-to-$70,000 payouts for Linux privilege-escalation exploits at hacking competitions, suggesting a hypothetical value of $18.9 million to $66.3 million if the same results were found manually.

An earlier GPT-5.6 Sol campaign found 166 unique privilege-escalation vulnerabilities in July for $513,690. A newer, unreleased cyber model found more than five times as many in October, even after the kernel had undergone months of fixes.

OpenAI also said its agents used at least 10 exploitation techniques unfamiliar to the researchers and outside experts they consulted—not merely existing tricks retrieved from training data.

AI companies finding critical bugs are just another proof of how systems are capable of performing advanced cybersecurity research achieving results that previously required significant human expertise.

Last week, Anthropic disclosed that an unreleased version of Claude had discovered and exploited serious vulnerabilities in widely used software, including Chrome and Linux. Some of those exploits could execute malicious code remotely with little or no user interaction.

The economics of this research were already changing before OpenAI's announcement.

Researchers at Vidoc Security reproduced several vulnerability discoveries associated with Anthropic's powerful Mythos AI using publicly accessible models for less than $30 per file.

Those tests showed that finding vulnerabilities was becoming increasingly accessible, although reproducing a flaw is different from developing a reliable exploit capable of compromising a protected system.

OpenAI's latest experiment goes further by automating much of that exploitation process, creating a potential advantage for defenders trying to patch software before criminals can take advantage of the same weaknesses.

The company warned that attackers with limited access to an outdated operating system may increasingly be able to escalate their privileges with AI assistance.

OpenAI is attempting to restrict access to its more capable cybersecurity models through verified security programs. Its Daybreak Red initiative provides approved defenders with access to specialized AI tools for vulnerability research, exploit testing, and mitigation development.

So, I feel like we are in a great position from a gain on sale perspective, a volume perspective, and whether the rates, our gain on sale is slightly higher or slightly lower than your range, but I definitely see it in that range. It is not going back to 60 or 70, like some of our competitors are at, trying to win loans and stay afloat.

New Accounting Seed Research Exposes the Hidden Workload Behind AI in Finance

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A survey of nearly 200 business and finance professionals at small and midsize companies finds fragmented data, constant output checks, and new governance work are severely limiting what AI delivers for the business

COLUMBIA, Md., Nov. 5, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Accounting Seed, the AI-native accounting platform built on Salesforce, released its latest research report, The Hidden Workload Behind AI Productivity in Finance. The survey found that 82% of finance team members at U.S. small and midsize companies would get more value from AI if their financial data were in one system.

Many businesses are expecting a leap in productivity from AI layered on top of financial systems that don't share data and lack the business context behind it. When the data underneath is scattered and stripped of meaning, AI inherits every gap, and finance teams are left to sort it out.

Scattered data and missing context leave finance teams cleaning up after AIAI is helping finance teams work faster, but it has also shifted new work onto them. When financial data is spread across disconnected tools, someone still has to pull the information together, decide which numbers to trust, and check AI's output before it can be used. Without access to a company's financial data, policies, and accounting practices, AI also lacks the context needed to provide insights that support strategic business decisions.

"When your financial data lives in six places, your AI has six places to be wrong," said Nasser Chanda, CEO of Accounting Seed. "You can give AI the skills of the smartest analyst in the world, but if it's working from scattered spreadsheets and three versions of the truth, someone still has to figure out which numbers to trust. That's the hidden workload. The next wave of productivity will come from AI that works from a single source of truth, understands how your business operates, and can deliver trustworthy strategic insights as a result."

Reducing the friction around AI in financeNearly all executives surveyed (98%) say they use AI in finance to increase their team's output, not reduce its size. But when employees spend their saved time preparing data, correcting results, and managing new governance requirements, that capacity never reaches the business.

Reducing that friction starts with the system underneath AI. When financial and operational data live in one system under one set of access controls, AI works from a single version of the truth, and teams spend less time cleaning up behind it.

"When accounting and operational data share one platform, AI can work with information across the business and understand the context behind it without teams having to piece it together first," said Ryan Sieve, CTO of Accounting Seed. "And when that access is governed by existing permissions and trust boundaries, companies can expand what AI does without creating a separate set of controls for every new tool."

The full report, The Hidden Workload Behind AI Productivity in Finance, includes findings across executives, finance leaders, finance team members, and Operations & IT professionals, showing where AI is delivering value, where it's creating new work, and why adding more AI tools won't fix a company's underlying data issues.

About Accounting SeedAccounting Seed gives fast-growing businesses a modern, AI-native accounting platform â comprehensive AR/AP automation, multi-dimensional reporting, and the flexibility to keep pace as business scales. Since 2011, the company has helped thousands of organizations run accounting where their business already operates, natively on Salesforce. A recognized Inc. 5000 honoree for seven years running, Accounting Seed is trusted by more than 60,000 active users across 30+ countries.
