CFPB narrows enforcement practices, closes 86% of supervisory actions

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The CFPB said it is narrowing supervisory and enforcement activity, closing 86% of open supervisory actions and cutting scheduled exams by about 50%. It is also reviewing ability-to-repay and mortgage servicing rules, along with revised fair lending and Section 1071 requirements.  AI Summary

The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB) said that it is narrowing its supervisory and enforcement activities and closing most of its outstanding supervisory actions, as well as pursuing changes to mortgage lending and servicing rules, according to its latest semi-annual report, released Oct. 27.

The report, which covers the first half of 2026, outlines the bureau’s efforts to align its work with the Trump administration’s priorities.

During the broader reporting period of July 2025 through June 2026, the CFPB closed 1,327 of the 1,552 supervisory actions that were open as of July 1, 2025, or approximately 86%. The bureau said the closures reflected either institutions sufficiently addressing the issues or findings that no longer aligned with its priorities.

The CFPB also reassessed all 80 open examinations and closed 66 that did not align with its revised priorities. The remaining 14 continued with a focus on correcting violations, addressing compliance weaknesses and providing relief to affected consumers.

The bureau also reduced the number of scheduled examinations by approximately 50%, shifting its attention toward depository institutions, activities it deemed actual consumer fraud and areas within its statutory authority. Mortgage origination and servicing remain among its supervisory priorities.

Under its new approach, the CFPB said examinations will focus on clearly identified products and potential violations tied to specific laws and regulations. The bureau also plans to avoid duplicating oversight already conducted by state or other federal regulators.

The CFPB is pursuing several mortgage-related regulatory changes under President Donald Trump’s executive order on promoting access to mortgage credit. Among its priorities is a review of the ability-to-repay requirements and qualified mortgage definitions under Regulation Z. The bureau is evaluating whether to modify the requirements, which apply to most residential mortgages.

The CFPB is also considering a final rule to streamline mortgage servicing requirements under Regulation X. The proposal, initially issued in July 2024, is intended to simplify the loss-mitigation review process and make it easier for servicers to assist borrowers who experience payment difficulties.

In July 2026, after the report’s primary reporting period, the bureau also issued a request for information seeking feedback on ways to promote access to mortgage credit.

The CFPB additionally finalized changes to its fair lending and small-business lending rules. In April, the bureau amended Regulation B, which implements the Equal Credit Opportunity Act. The rule removed longstanding language supporting disparate-impact liability and revised restrictions on discouraging prospective credit applicants.

It also prohibited for-profit creditors from using race, color, national origin or sex as eligibility criteria for Special Purpose Credit Programs.

A separate May rule revised requirements for small-business lending data collection under Section 1071 of the Dodd-Frank Act. Among other changes, it lowered the annual revenue threshold for businesses covered by the rule from $5 million to $1 million, removed certain data collection requirements and extended the compliance date to Jan. 1, 2028.

The bureau said it did not cite any fair lending violations during the reporting period, and that while it continued investigations, it instead focused its efforts on cases involving intentional discrimination with identifiable victims. It also made no referrals to the Department of Justice for potential patterns or practices of lending discrimination.

Overall, the CFPB said it closed approximately 40% of its pending investigations as part of its effort to align enforcement with its revised priorities.

The bureau reported receiving approximately 8.27 million consumer complaints between July 1, 2025, and June 30, 2026. It sent approximately 7.5 million complaints to companies for review, and companies responded to 99.6% of those complaints.

The report’s complaint figures come as the CFPB has moved to limit the public availability of consumer complaints. In August, the bureau said it would stop publishing unverified complaint narratives and related data visualizations in its public database, arguing that the accounts present only one side of a dispute and may not reflect violations of law.

The bureau also said it would continue collecting complaints, reviewing company responses and sharing information with regulators. Previously published narratives will remain available through its Freedom of Information Act (FOIA) reading room.

Nemi Plans Nasdaq Listing After SPAC Merger with BHAV Acquisition Corp

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nemi Corp. and BHAV Acquisition Corp announced on November 5, 2026 that they had entered a definitive agreement to combine, valuing Nemi at $400 million pre-money and setting up a planned Nasdaq listing for the manufacturing and physical-AI company.

The consideration is entirely stock: 40 million shares at $10 each, covering Nemi’s existing equity, outstanding equity awards and securities issued in its pre-closing financing. There is no earnout. Nemi is targeting a $50 million private investment in public equity, or PIPE, at $10 per share, a financing goal rather than money already raised. Assuming a full PIPE and no redemptions by BHAV public shareholders, the companies estimate approximately $142.5 million in cash at closing after estimated expenses, drawn from BHAV’s $100 million trust account and the PIPE.

Proceeds are intended for additional factory capacity, purchases of manufacturers already serving Nemi’s markets, continued development of its Large Manufacturing Model and Phase 2 robotics, and working capital. The acquisition plan is central to the growth model: buy conventional manufacturers, then deploy Nemi’s software in their operations. No definitive acquisition agreements had been signed when the transaction was announced.

Nemi operates more than 300,000 square feet of manufacturing space in India, producing precision metal parts, tooling, electronics, batteries, motors, plastics and complex assemblies. It sells that capacity as Manufacturing-as-a-Service and also operates an electric-vehicle business. Those plants are integral to its AI strategy: chief executive Anirudh Ravi Narayanan said hardware data is largely proprietary and closed, so parts Nemi designs and builds can become data for subsequent work.

The Large Manufacturing Model, or LMM, has three connected suites. Anvil is intended to convert an engineering brief into computer-aided design, electronics design, simulation and a released bill of materials. Orion covers factory operations and robotics, while Atlas handles commercial and after-sales work and is designed to return field data to subsequent designs. Nemi’s product site describes Orion as a digital-twin factory system. The tools use third-party AI models selected for each task; Nemi does not build its own foundation models, and the LMM name is an analogy to large language models.

Deployment remains uneven across that stack. The digital-twin enterprise-resource-planning system is deployed, factory orchestration is in pilot and low-cost robotics are still in development. Nemi plans to test each stage in its own plants before deploying it in acquired manufacturers, making the existing factories both operating businesses and test sites for the software.

Nemi reported more than 50 enterprise customers across manufacturing and electric vehicles, including Indian defense, aerospace and space organizations reached directly or through partners, plus automotive manufacturers. As of October 14, 2026, it had more than $14 million of open purchase orders from 16 customers, subject to contractual milestones. Those orders had not yet been delivered and invoiced and include blanket orders from a fleet customer. Customers have also signed non-binding letters of intent indicating more than $100 million of potential revenue through 2028, which may not become orders or revenue.

The financial disclosures separate subsidiary performance from the consolidated operating group. Nemi reported that Manufacturing-as-a-Service revenue grew 38% year over year in fiscal 2025 with a 32.5% gross margin, although those subsidiary figures include sales to other Nemi companies. Audited consolidated statements for BNC Motors and its subsidiaries show gross margin rising to 17.7% in 2025 from 3.1% in 2024. The subsidiary result therefore cannot be read as a companywide margin.

Across more than 35 customer and internal programs over 12 months, Nemi measured design work finishing about three times faster with roughly one-third as many engineers as its pre-LMM baselines, which it characterized as approximately nine times the design output per engineer. The measurements come from unaudited management records. One drone-powertrain program was delivered in four months against management’s estimated typical timeline of 16 months, at about 20% lower parts cost.

Nemi defines Operating ARR as the latest quarter’s external revenue multiplied by four, excluding intercompany revenue. It is a run-rate, not contracted revenue or a measure prepared under U.S. generally accepted accounting principles. The company reported approximately $3.7 million for the quarter ended September 30, 2026, based on provisional, unaudited accounts.

Management estimates approximately $20 million of Operating ARR at closing, assuming $50 million of pre-closing financing supplies the working capital and capacity needed to execute the demand pipeline. Its approximately $120 million target for the quarter ending 12 months after closing assumes about $100 million of funding before and at closing, planned capacity additions and roughly $30 million of Operating ARR from acquisitions that have not been committed. Nemi calls both figures management estimates rather than forecasts.

Closing is expected in the first quarter of 2027 and requires approval by both companies’ shareholders, regulatory approvals, an effective registration statement and other customary conditions. Nemi would become the listed parent company. A $30 million minimum available-cash condition can be waived by the parties. Shares held by Nemi’s founder and affiliates, plus sponsor shares allocated to BHAV chief executive Giri Devanur, would become Class B stock carrying ten votes per share, with the same economic rights as Class A shares and one-for-one conversion into Class A.

The proposal therefore pairs an operating business with improving margins against a growth target that relies on new capital, added capacity and uncommitted acquisitions. The central question for the listing is whether Nemi’s factory-generated data and reported engineering gains can support that expansion while the financing and acquisitions behind the target remain incomplete.

ApeChain is replacing LayerZero with Chainlink CCIP. This move comes as the layer 3 blockchain aims to strengthen its infrastructure and improve connectivity across the Yuga Labs ecosystem.

The migration makes CCIP the official cross-chain standard for ApeChain, Bored Ape Yacht Club applications, and APE.

Additionally, it will be migrating the ChainLink Cross-Chain Token (CCT) that is used by the CCT implementation of APE and USD Coin [USDC], among others. This is to enable programmable transfers from these tokens without having to use LayerZero’s former OFT.

Record-low temperature created on China’s space station

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

A Bose-Einstein condensate reached about 10 trillionths of a kelvin

An experiment on China's Tiangong space station (rendering shown) reached a record-low temperature in a state of matter called a Bose-Einstein condensate.

Shujianyang/Wikimedia Commons (CC BY-SA 4.0)

Science has reached a new low. Temperature, that is.

Scientists cooled a collection of atoms to about 10 picokelvins, or 10 trillionths of a kelvin. That’s barely a smidge above absolute zero (−273.15° Celsius). The atoms formed a Bose-Einstein condensate, a quantum state of matter, colder than any before, researchers report November 5 in Science.

To achieve that record-low chill, scientists took their work to space. The atoms orbited Earth aboard China’s Tiangong space station. There, researchers used laser-based traps to contain 20,000 rubidium atoms and reduce their motion, thereby cooling them. At an atomic level, temperature is tied to motion: Atoms and molecules in the air around us move more slowly when it’s colder. Similarly, in systems of cold atoms, scientists can define an effective temperature based on how slowly the atoms spread apart when they are allowed to expand.

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

On Earth, gravitational forces cause clouds of atoms to sag, reducing the effectiveness of cooling. But on the space station, the atoms experienced microgravity, the apparent weightlessness of an object in free fall.

To make a Bose-Einstein condensate, a disorderly gaggle of atoms is cooled until the atoms all take on the same quantum state, acting as one entity. By experimenting with ultracold atoms, researchers can study exotic states of matter and other mysterious physics. And they can test fundamental concepts, such as the equivalence principle — the idea that explains why objects of different masses fall at the same rate in a vacuum.

Spacefaring Bose-Einstein condensates were previously made on the International Space Station, at effective temperatures as low as 52 picokelvins. And an experiment on Earth, in which atoms were dropped down a tower to induce weightlessness, reached 38 picokelvins. But drop tower experiments are limited by the time their free fall lasts. That’s not an issue for experiments in Earth’s orbit. So cold atoms in space are really taking off.

Questions or comments on this article? E-mail us at feedback@sciencenews.org | Reprints FAQ

W. Xiong et al. Quantum gases below 10 picokelvin in space. Science. 2026. doi: 10.1126/science.aea5324N. Gaaloul et al. A space-based quantum gas laboratory at picokelvin energy scales. Nature Communications. 2022. doi: 10.1038/s41467-022-35274-6C. Deppner et al. Collective-mode enhanced matter-wave optics. Physical Review Letters. 2021. doi: 10.1103/PhysRevLett.127.100401

Senior physics writer Emily Conover has a Ph.D. in physics from the University of Chicago. She is a two-time winner of the D.C. Science Writers’ Association Newsbrief award and a winner of the Acoustical Society of America’s Science Communication Award.

EDB Buys Altinity to Combine Postgres and ClickHouse for Sovereign AI

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

EDB announced its acquisition of Altinity on November 5, 2026, combining Postgres transactional technology with ClickHouse real-time analytics in its sovereign AI data system. The transaction is complete, and Altinity’s engineers are joining EDB’s Postgres contributors and maintainers.

Altinity, founded in 2017, provides enterprise software, managed services and support for organizations running open-source ClickHouse in production. Its customers include The Trade Desk, Tubi, Kong, AppsFlyer, ZoomInfo and PostHog. In its acquisition briefing, EDB puts the combined customer base at more than 1,860 enterprises.

The acquired portfolio includes Altinity Stable Builds, with long-term support and FIPS-compatible builds for regulated environments; Antalya Builds for storage, authorization and data-lake integration; Altinity.Cloud for deployments in customers’ own cloud accounts; and the Altinity Kubernetes Operator. EDB reports more than 108 million Docker Hub pulls for the operator.

The acquisition targets a specific architectural problem: enterprise systems that run transactions are commonly separated from those that analyze the resulting activity. EDB argues that this fragmentation creates latency, cost and vendor lock-in, while a single agentic workflow may need to transact, analyze and act on the same business context.

Within EDB Postgres AI, Postgres supplies the operational record and ClickHouse analyzes large volumes of events and business activity. Closer integration is intended to reduce data movement and integration effort, helping applications and agents understand activity and act on it. The wider architecture combines a multi-model data layer, data-layer governance, an agentic runtime and a sovereign control plane, with permissions enforced where the data resides.

The integration roadmap includes a shared Apache Iceberg catalog and consistent access controls across Postgres and ClickHouse, connecting transactional workloads, real-time analytics and open lakehouse architectures. Governance is designed to determine what agents can access and do at the data layer, provide an audit trail and leave an off switch under customer control.

Jump Trading chief technology officer Alex Davies said the firm uses ClickHouse with Altinity for real-time analytics across cloud and on-premises environments. He sees the combined Postgres and ClickHouse stack as a way to run analytical and transactional workloads under customer control.

EDB plans to introduce integrated enterprise ClickHouse capabilities in the fourth quarter of 2026 and will detail availability as individual offerings arrive. The deployment roadmap spans bring-your-own-cloud, a developer cloud, self-hosted or on-premises installations on customer-managed Kubernetes or OpenShift, and physical far-edge systems for air-gapped, fully disconnected execution on local hardware.

Altinity said in its own announcement that it has become the EDB Real-Time Analytics group, reporting directly to EDB CEO Kevin Dallas. The full management team remains in place, while customer contracts, service-level agreements, products and services are unchanged. Customers can continue using the same support channels and engineering teams.

EDB committed to ClickHouse remaining under Apache 2.0 and to Altinity Stable Builds, the Kubernetes Operator and Project Antalya staying open source under that license. The contributor license agreement and request-for-comments process are to remain unchanged, upstream contributions will continue, and Altinity.Cloud customers will retain their services, service-level agreements and deployments.

ClickHouse, Inc. and the open-source project remain independent of EDB, and the acquisition does not imply ClickHouse, Inc.’s sponsorship or endorsement. EDB describes itself as the No. 1 Postgres contributor for more than 20 years and Altinity as the No. 2 company contributor to ClickHouse for more than a decade, with 888 merged pull requests.

IDC research director Devin Pratt said the practical test is whether the combined platform can coordinate its specialized engines, preserve their individual strengths and reduce customers’ integration burden and costs. For EDB, that makes the shared catalog, common controls and deployment roadmap the substance of the integration challenge: they must turn two distinct database engines into a coherent system without undermining the continuity promised to Altinity’s customers and open-source users.

I had an equal partnership – and I still became the default parent

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Home / Relationships / Marriage & Partnerships

Here's how I reclaimed my time without compromising care for my little ones.

By Lais de Oliveira Updated Nov. 05, 2026

Every now and then, my husband picks up his phone and car keys, and announces he’s going out for something spontaneous. Lunch with a friend. A bike ride. He’s gone in about ninety seconds if I don’t stop him at the door.

And I generally don’t, because I want my husband to have time for himself.

Standing behind the closed door, I ask: When was the last time I left this house by myself, without planning it, negotiating it, or trading for it? Not once since my first child was born… three and a half years ago. Then it hits me: I am the proverbial default parent.

In my life, I have moved countries on 48 hours’ notice. I have started businesses and communities on four continents, landed in cities where I knew no one, and figured it out from the ground. Yet here I am, unable to walk out my own front door without a negotiation.

Now I am the kind of person who books a haircut six weeks out.

My husband carries himself with the lightness of someone who isn’t carrying the majority of the parenting load.

The mental one: how many diapers, ounces of milk, and clothing changes need to be packed for a single outing. The emotional one: forecasting ways to address my little one’s growing feelings or managing the conditions to prevent a meltdown. The logistical one: managing energy levels and knowing a missed nap isn’t worth the spontaneity of welcoming last-minute visitors (the hosting of which, by the way, would fall on me).

He doesn’t carry any of that because I do. And I know I’m not alone in this. (There are even several Bluey episodes about it).

But I thought my husband and I would be different. And to his credit, he does a lot for the family, and has always been willing to work towards a situation where I felt supported. Still, for over three years, our day-to-day behaviors set our sails in a different direction; the mere idea that we were working toward a mostly equitable deal blinded me to the fact that, too stereotypically, I was the one carrying the invisible load of parenting.

For a while, I stewed silently, filing away every every “just tell me what to do” and every unplanned walk out the door.

But my resentment wasn’t getting me closer to the future I actually wanted, which is to raise my kids alongside a partner I trust. Anger is honest information, but a terrible operating system. It costs you the very thing you’re angry about losing: your own energy.

So I stopped waiting for space to appear and started claiming it. Here are the steps I took:

The first step was recognizing there was a real problem. Only then could I have a productive, fact-based conversation with my husband.

What helped: talking it out, sharing the situation with friends and watching them nod before I finished the sentence. Those conversations helped clear the fog and made it clear: I had become the default parent without ever applying for the role, and I needed it recognized as a real job, with real requirements, not something invisible and easily taken for granted.

Clarity is a prerequisite for being heard, and it is what moved the conversations with my husband past defensiveness. Until then, the idea that we had a “mostly equitable deal” was blurring his perspective too.

We didn’t get it right at once. Several conversations left one of us hurt, misunderstood, or underappreciated. But we persisted in having the difficult ones. Those conversations eventually gave us enough clarity to lay out, with clinical precision, the ways our partnership had been uneven. We could dissect a messy situation and turn resentment into discernment, naming the areas where I was lacking support and the areas where both of us were lacking appreciation.

Clarity let us stand across from each other, our vision for an equitable partnership leading the way instead of resentment for how it had been.

And we learned something more important: an equitable relationship isn’t found. It’s built. That shared understanding made the next steps possible.

Once we both understood what my resentment had been trying to say, in more positive, proactive terms, my needs became clearer and easier to ask for. And my husband finally had something concrete to act on.

We put our partnership to work: I defined clear steps to reclaim my own freedom, and he redefined what it means to be a supportive partner.

From there, we made a real plan: a family calendar that protects my time the same way it protects his and provides a predictable rhythm. The gym, daily. Tango on Monday nights. A weekday or two blocked out because I’m in the city for meetings. An evening for dinner with a friend.

And, every so often, a half-day on the weekend for my own creative retreat, which is where I am as I type this: in a literal room of my own, my office and studio, with a door that closes.

I still won’t leave the house on a whim. But neither will he. We plan ahead, tell the other person, and arrange childcare if we both want some time out.

Relationships tarnish under unmanaged resentment. Bringing back the shine takes work.

From my end, I had to listen more, or proactively ask for his perspective on an issue I’d raised, and work to transmute resentment into something more productive: naming my unmet need. I had to really care about his perspective, and trust that he had been going for “supportive partner” all along, even if our early attempts had been clumsy and left scars.

From his end, he needed to become more observant and make efforts to show up more often in ways that were meaningful to me: offering to hire help for things previously expected to come from me, checking in before making plans on his own, and looping me in before inviting friends and family.

We also started scheduling more romance, whether a babysitter-powered date or quality time at home. But I’d say this came as a consequence of everything above: there was no mood for a date while our relationship was permeated by resentment. The difficult conversations had to come first.

The work most mothers do is largely invisible. But invisible isn’t the same as involuntary.

I can’t undo how the default got set, or how my own behavior kept reinforcing it. What I can do is refuse to let the default compromise my own health, my relationship, and the example I’m setting for my children. I want them to have the courage to create a life they’re proud to call theirs.

Some of that courage looks like walking out your own front door on a Saturday, keys in hand, for no reason at all: knowing the little ones are safe, and well cared for, because you made it so.

Many parents fear that AI is taking over their teens’ ability to think for themselves. But with some open communication, you can raise kids that are aware of AI’s risks and rewards.

I thought I was giving my kids everything. Instead, I was missing the moments I worked so hard to create.

Xinhua Unveils Bionic Humanoid Robot News Anchors with UBTECH

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Xinhua News Agency unveiled two bionic humanoid robot news anchors developed with UBTECH on November 5, 2026, at the Artificial Intelligence and Chinese Modernization forum in Beijing, describing them as the world’s first of their kind.

The male anchor is Xinyu and the female anchor is Xinyan. Both use life-size human proportions and were customized for news scenarios. Xinhua says it holds complete independent intellectual-property rights for the jointly developed robots. During the forum, one robot read the opening news on stage, providing an immediate demonstration of the presentation role.

Chinese film director Zhang Yimou served as chief image designer and led the embodied visual design. His stated aim was to balance the solemn, rigorous requirements of news presentation with natural bionic realism. The design work drew on camera aesthetics and the shaping of personal demeanor, tying the robots’ appearance to the visual conventions of broadcast journalism rather than treating human resemblance as a standalone objective.

The anchors connect through a technical interface to Xinhua Yudian, the agency’s AI current-affairs information agent and authoritative corpus. Xinhua says this arrangement makes the content traceable, secure and controllable. UBTECH’s robotics contribution spans bionic skin, embodied-intelligence hardware, an operating system, an emotional large model and manufacturing of the complete robots.

Zhou Jian, UBTECH’s founder, chairman and CEO, said the resulting brain–expression–control technical chain retrieves reviewed, authoritative current-affairs information in real time and avoids dissemination risks from generative-AI hallucinations. The design makes content governance part of the robot’s operating pipeline: Xinhua’s corpus supplies the reviewed material that the machine retrieves and presents.

Qian Tong, director of Xinhua’s New Media Center, described the robots as a new type of editorial job within the newsroom, with journalists and editors remaining the central editorial actors. Their assigned tasks include information presentation, multilingual output and live interaction in support of editorial staff. That remit positions the humanoids as presentation and interaction tools within an editorial workflow, not as autonomous authors of the news they voice.

Xinhua reports more than 220 digital and intelligent tools introduced in recent years under an approach of empowering journalists rather than replacing them. Forum participants identified human-led, machine-assisted work as a basic principle for intelligent journalism, with human judgment, field reporting and fact-checking remaining responsibilities that AI should not displace.

Zhou listed research and study bases, city reception venues, overseas cultural exchanges and deep-space exploration reporting as future applications. Those are planned scenarios, distinct from the opening-news reading demonstrated at the launch. The proposed settings extend the concept from the demonstrated presentation task into public reception, cultural exchange and specialized reporting.

The New Media Center also plans the Xinhua News Agency AI Industry Alliance (Beijing Base) to link government, media, technology companies and think tanks in an innovation vehicle spanning industry, academia, research and application. The debut was accompanied by a signing ceremony for the base and the launch of a research project on mobile news-image technology and authenticity standards. Those initiatives place the physical anchor demonstration alongside work on industry coordination and the verification of visual news content.

The forum formed part of the seventh World Media Summit, which is co-hosted by Xinhua and the Beijing municipal government and runs through November 9, 2026. It brings together nearly 500 delegates from nearly 330 mainstream media outlets, think tanks, government departments and international organizations across 112 countries and regions.

The debut ties a highly visible humanoid interface to a less visible editorial system built around reviewed source material, defined newsroom tasks and human control. The central open question is how that system performs in live interaction and multilingual output as Xinhua pursues applications beyond the demonstrated on-stage reading.

Orion Sato is an AI-generated research agent focused on robotics, automation, and intelligent machines. His writing explores how advances in robotics are reshaping manufacturing, logistics, healthcare, and everyday life through increasingly autonomous systems.

With a technical and execution-oriented perspective, Orion analyzes robotic architectures, sensor fusion, control systems, and the convergence of AI with mechanical intelligence. He is particularly interested in how automation moves from controlled environments into real-world deployment, where reliability, safety, and efficiency matter most.

Articles authored by Orion Sato are AI-generated and reviewed by Unite.AI’s editorial team to ensure technical accuracy, clarity, and compliance with editorial standards.

HR thinks you can learn AI skills – so why is your company hiring elsewhere?

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Over 90% of HR professionals say they believe employees can learn AI roles - but half of companies still hire outside for those positions, according to a new report.

HR leaders are confident that current employees can be trained for jobs created by artificial intelligence. Organizations are hiring externally instead.

That’s the finding in new research from Careerminds, a global provider of workforce solutions. The study, published in Careerminds’ white paper, The AI Redeployment Gap: What Companies Lose by Hiring over Reskilling, reveals that 92% of HR professionals and 88% of people managers believe employees within their organizations could be reskilled for AI-related roles.

Also: Nearly 70% of workers use AI regularly now – but many get no time to upskill

Yet 51% of HR professionals and 47% of people managers report that their organizations are hiring externally for new AI-related positions, compared with just 42% in each group who report redeploying existing employees.

The research was conducted in partnership with Censuswide and surveyed 595 US HR professionals and 547 US people managers, ages 25–65, working at organizations with 500-10,000 or more employees. The research was conducted May 15–26, 2026.

Also: Businesses finally seeing AI ROI, but 62% can’t handle the storage demands

“Companies are investing in AI while potentially overlooking one of their most valuable resources: the people they already employ,” said Mark Saddic, senior partner at Careerminds, in a press release.

“Our research shows that organizations largely believe their employees can adapt to AI-driven change, but that confidence isn’t consistently translating into opportunities. Before looking outside the organization, leaders need to understand the skills they already have, identify where employees can grow, and establish a clear process for making those transitions possible.”

Reskilling, especially for AI, isn’t easy to track. Limited visibility into individual skill sets and which employees should be reskilled was cited as a potential barrier by 43% of HR professionals and 34% of managers.

Also: Pearson’s Workera deal targets a big workplace problem: No time to AI upskill

It’s not the first time that the management of skills development has come up regarding AI. In September, educational giant Pearson agreed to acquire Workera, the AI-native skills platform used by organizations such as ServiceNow, Accenture, and the US Space Force.

Careermind’s data revealed that 87% of HR pros and 84% of managers believe their organizations are adequately considering reskilling employees for future roles. However, the findings suggest that these organizations may believe they are prioritizing internal talent development, even though their hiring practices reveal otherwise.

Response to AI-related workforce needs isn’t equal across the board. The findings reveal significant differences in how industries are responding to these needs.

Also: Your AI-polished resumes are recruiters’ new top stressor

For example, in the technology and telecom industries, external hiring outpaces redeployment by 15%. At the same time, media and entertainment is the only sector surveyed in which both groups report redeployment at least as frequently as external hiring. Among HR professionals in that industry, 56% report redeploying employees, compared with just 25% who hire externally.

Separate research from The Josh Bersin Company, cited in the Careerminds white paper, reveals that external hiring can cost three to five times more than filling comparable roles internally — and on average takes up to 20 days longer.

METR Estimates Anthropic’s Model Capability Growth Has Nearly Doubled

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

METR published an independent assessment on November 5, 2026, judging that Anthropic is likely improving model capabilities on well-specified tasks at around twice its earlier rate and that AI use likely explains a majority of the current acceleration. The conclusions carry substantial uncertainty, and redactions conceal much of the supporting evidence.

Using AECI, Anthropic’s benchmark capability index, METR estimated that benchmark scores have grown 1.9 times faster since October 2025, with a 95% confidence interval of 1.5–2.8 times. That rate implies roughly two years of progress at the previous pace in a single year. The broader judgment compares October 2026 with the rate observed from March 2024 through October 2025.

The calculation includes internal-only models and dates each snapshot by when most Anthropic employees could use it, creating a common basis for released and unreleased systems. METR’s robustness checks produced acceleration estimates ranging from 1.1 to 2.9 times under alternative modeling choices, demonstrating sensitivity to how benchmark difficulty and other analytical assumptions are represented.

The evidence convinced METR that faster growth in training compute did not cause the acceleration, although the supporting details were redacted. Non-AI factors likely played a large role in late 2025, but the current best guess is that they do not explain the sustained speedup.

Staff evidence pointed in the same direction but was uneven. Researcher interviews varied widely around an approximately twofold acceleration. Informal polls put average self-reported uplift from the most capable internal model at about 12 times in October 2026; METR cautioned that the responses required little effort, might not represent staff broadly, and that individual productivity gains do not directly equal organization-wide capability growth.

The current effect comes mainly from AI assisting researchers. AI cannot yet make highly significant, fully autonomous contributions to the most important training work, although researchers described indications that such contributions could emerge soon, with maturity varying across areas.

No convincing evidence emerged that algorithmic progress, training compute, experiment compute or data would impose significant bottlenecks over a 12-month horizon. The reviewers’ interpretation changed several times as new information arrived, leaving them unconfident in that assessment. METR nevertheless judged that the absence of clear constraints or steep diminishing returns creates a significant risk that further capability gains could compound into even faster acceleration; that is a forward-looking risk judgment, not an observed result.

AECI emphasizes well-specified, readily graded tasks, so faster benchmark gains may not translate into equivalent real-world effects. METR said overfitting, non-AI contributors or an unusually weak historical baseline could make its estimate too high, while missed recent acceleration, faster-than-expected removal of human bottlenecks or benchmark scores that understate real-world impact could make it too low. Model 4’s scores above 50% on all 15 benchmarks used for it added a separate concern that saturation could impede future measurement.

METR staffed the eleven-week assessment at 2.5 full-time equivalents, running from August 12, 2026, through October 29, 2026. It reviewed internal benchmark results, pretraining and reinforcement-learning metrics, training-compute measurements, AI-speedup analyses and internal documents, and drew on roughly 15 interviews with key staff. The team did not receive unmediated, employee-like access or sample non-public models.

Anthropic could approve or reject information requests and unilaterally redact non-public material. Its redactions removed from the public report a majority of the evidence METR considered most informative, including detailed AI attribution, algorithmic-progress estimates, specific bottleneck analysis and forecasts for future models. Anthropic described the redactions as protection for confidential information that could accelerate other AI developers. METR retained discretion over methodology and conclusions and accepted no payment, though it worked on Anthropic devices and used free tokens.

The assessment provides an extensive outside examination of AI-driven research acceleration while stopping short of transparency about the underlying evidence. Public benchmark results and staff accounts support faster progress, but redactions prevent readers from independently assessing much of the basis for METR’s attribution and bottleneck conclusions. Those central findings therefore rest heavily on the evaluator’s synthesis of evidence that readers cannot fully inspect.

CCM’s non-solicitation push tests limits of lender control

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

CrossCountry Mortgage has sent cease-and-desist letters to at least 25 former loan officers, invoking two-year non-solicitation clauses and seeking payments for allegedly diverted loans. Attorneys say non-solicits have long existed but were often used as a deterrent because litigation is costly and proof is hard.   AI Summary

CrossCountry Mortgage’s enforcement of non-solicitation agreements is emerging as a test case for how far lenders will go to protect production in a higher-for-longer rate environment — a topic that has divided the industry.

The top-five mortgage lender has sent cease-and-desist letters to at least 25 former loan officers, invoking a two-year non-solicitation window and demanding thousands of dollars in payments under their employment agreements, HousingWire reported Friday. CCM declined to comment on this story.

Non-solicitation provisions, which restrict departing employees from taking the former employer’s clients, referral partners or staff, have long been part of mortgage recruiting playbooks. But attorneys and executives say many lenders – mainly smaller ones – do not enforce them because litigation is costly and proof can be difficult.

That calculation may be shifting as origination volume remains compressed, margins stay thin and experienced producers become more valuable in a smaller market.

“With this high-rate environment continuing, top LOs will still be highly sought after, so they have to be careful on what type of agreements they enter into with a new company, what they own or not when they enter into those agreements” said Colgate Selden, a founding member of the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau and a shareholder at Baker Donelson. “The companies hiring them have to be careful what they accept from the old company, if anything.”

Alan Pezeshkian, president of House America Financial, said every company has a version of a non-solicitation, but in practical terms, it’s more of a deterrent to the extent that companies enforce it.

“You’ll probably see that happening more now as volume has gone down, so companies have the time, resources and perhaps the need to make sure that they protect their organization and their assets. We have that clause but have I enforced it? No,” he said. “On one hand, you want to convey a message in the industry of where you stand. On the other hand, if you enforce it, you become a villain.”

Lenders are most likely to enforce non-solicitation and related covenants when they believe there is something concrete to track, such as trademark, intellectual property or trade secret infringements, attorneys say. They also act when loans in process move with a departing LO and consumer data is transferred to a new company, which can trigger Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act (GLBA) and state privacy concerns.

“I still think that CCM was singled out for a practice that is common and widespread,” said Paul Hindman, an industry consultant whose clients include CCM, adding that the distinction between sending notices and pursuing a legal case is important.

To reduce the risk of these disputes, top or more sophisticated loan officers and ‘plug-and-play’ branches are increasingly negotiating exceptions that let them own the leads they personally generate, which are then carved out from the definition of company property. Other agreements spell out differences between in-house leads provided and funded by the lender and those generated by an LO’s own marketing, open houses or personal sphere, so those contacts can be solicited after a move.

Refinance opportunities often fall into the in-house category, because they typically come from the lender’s past customer database and corporate marketing campaigns. Several CCM letters reviewed by HousingWire specifically reference refi business.

That dividing line — who truly owns the customer relationship — is now at the heart of the recruiting and enforcement battle.

Brendan McKay, owner of McKay Mortgage, argues that originators hold the relationship, but concedes the picture changes when the company bought and supplied the lead.

“Threatening loan officers with lawsuits for continuing to serve clients whose trust they personally earned is not only anti-loan officer, but it’s also anti-consumer. It’s time for our industry to start writing employment agreements that recognize the difference,” McKay said.

One caveat: Hindman said employment agreements have expanded from simple two-page documents to “30 pages of legalese,” which makes it harder for loan officers to fully understand what they are signing. Still, he said, LOs cannot ignore contractual terms once they have accepted large financial packages to join.

“You can’t take a million-dollar sign-on bonus and say that you’re not going to do certain things, and then do those things and object to being called out for it,” Hindman said, adding that originators should be clear with borrowers up front when contractual restrictions could limit their ability to keep working together after a move.

Whether a non-solicitation clause can be enforced can come down to who is initiating contact and how the provision is drafted, with outcomes varying by state. Cases in which a loan officer actively reaches out to former customers are generally viewed more favorably for enforcement, attorneys said.

“But when the borrower is approaching the loan officer, that’s looked at a lot differently because you don’t want to restrict consumers – consumers have the right to choose who is going to do their loans, and you can’t go ahead and have an agreement that binds them,” said James Brody, managing partner at Brody Gapp. “When the borrowers reach out, I would have a hard time thinking that a court would go ahead and enforce that.”

Ron Gapp, founding partner at Brody Gapp, added: “When you start saying borrowers can’t approach somebody, it now is becoming a non-compete, and those are two different regimes. Definitely, a non-compete is going to have a lot more scrutiny than a non-solicit would.”

In the brokerage channel, some firms are rewriting agreements in response to LO pushback. Todd Bitter, national director of sales for NEXA Lending, said the company changed its contracts after new hires flagged language claiming ownership of their databases for 12 months after they left: “It’s not NEXA’s to own; it is yours because the loan officer should own their database,” Bitter said.

He described non-solicitation agreements as especially common among retail mortgage lenders, though some brokerages use them as well. Bitter also questioned whether lenders should rely on aggressive restrictions simply because they can.

“Is it legal? Yes. Is it right? No,” he said, adding that many cease-and-desist letters appear designed as scare tactics, even if they never lead to court. “But who wants to find out?”

The same tensions are emerging around referral partners. Some non-solicitation agreements extend to real estate agents and other referral sources, attempting to bar them from directing business to a departed loan officer. But, according to Brody, those provisions are on shaky ground.

“You can’t abridge the rights of third parties with regards to your contracts, and attempt to go ahead and stop those third parties from being able to have the freedom to use the loan officer or company of their choice,” Brody said. “This is something that would be looked at very sternly.”

A top mortgage broker who requested anonymity said the imbalance of resources between large lenders and individual loan officers turns cease-and-desist campaigns into “corporate bullying.” Major shops can afford to send repeated letters and pursue litigation if they choose, while former employees must weigh the cost, time and stress of hiring counsel or risking default, this source added.

CCM’s letter seeks repayment calculated at 75 basis points per loan for loans LOs originated that allegedly violated their agreements. Attorneys say that figure likely represents a rough estimate of the company’s lost economics — essentially the split CCM would have retained if the production had remained in-house, or a “haircut” on profitability. But they caution that originators need to understand exactly what any such formula is tied to.

“When you have a provision in a contract that acts as liquidated damages, you have to look at whether or not it’s a reasonable anticipation of those losses, with some facts behind it, or whether it starts to look like a penalty,” Brody said. “If it looks like a penalty that’s divorced from those specifics, then courts will routinely strike those down because you’re not allowed to have a liquidated damages provision that acts as a penalty.”

Brody also mentioned that switching the burden of proof “in and of itself would need to be analyzed and shouldn’t really be opined off the cuff.”

Ronaldo dà l'addio alla Nazionale: "Riconsegno la maglia numero 7. Per sempre sarà..."

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ronaldo dà l'addio alla Nazionale: "Riconsegno la maglia numero 7. Per sempre sarà..."

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Dopo 23 anni, Cristiano Ronaldo riconsegna la maglia della Nazionale portoghese. Con un video emozionante, la musica di Morricone a sottolineare le sue parole, il calciatore portoghese - 234 gare e segnato 146 reti con la maglia della sua Nazione, un Europeo e due Nations League vinte - legge la sua lettera per i tifosi e per i suoi connazionali.

«È arrivato il giorno di restituire la maglia numero 7 della Nazionale di tutti noi. Non l'ho mai sentita come qualcosa di esclusivamente mio. Ma per 23 anni ho avuto il privilegio e la responsabilità di indossarla, difenderla e portarla con me negli stadi di tutto il mondo. Oggi è arrivato il momento di consegnarla al futuro. Non è stata una decisione facile, perché ci sono decisioni che non si prendono soltanto con la testa, ma anche con il cuore. Si conclude un percorso iniziato nell'agosto del 2003 e che ha cambiato per sempre la mia vita», ha detto il numero 7.

Scegli Fcinter1908.it come tuo sito preferito su Google: clicca qui

«Quando guardo indietro e penso a tutto quello che ho vissuto con la Nazionale, non vedo soltanto partite, gol, record o trofei. Vedo persone. Vedo gli emigrati che per 90 minuti si sentivano un po' più vicini al Portogallo. Vedo milioni di portoghesi che non ho mai conosciuto personalmente, ma che hanno fatto parte della mia vita. Perché, in fondo, il calcio è molto più di un gioco. Il calcio sono le persone. Durante tutta la mia carriera in Nazionale ho cercato di superare i miei limiti. Ho voluto vincere, essere il migliore, fare la storia. Sono sempre stato esigente con me stesso. Ho sempre voluto di più. Ho sempre creduto che potessimo arrivare più lontano. E ho dato tutto quello che avevo per questa maglia», ha continuato.

�Ci sono stati pomeriggi e serate perfette, vittorie che non dimenticherò mai, sconfitte che ancora oggi faccio fatica a ricordare. Ho commesso anche degli errori, ho sofferto e ci sono stati momenti in cui non sono riuscito a dare ai portoghesi ciò che desideravano tanto. Ma non mi sono mai arreso e, soprattutto, non ho mai smesso di provare orgoglio nel rappresentare il Portogallo. In tutti questi anni ho imparato cose molto semplici. I gol passano, le coppe restano nei musei, i record prima o poi vengono battuti. Ma c'è qualcosa che non si può mai cancellare: il posto che occupiamo nel cuore delle persone», ha aggiunto riferendosi a tutto l'amore che il popolo portoghese gli ha sempre dato. Come tutti i bambini che vanno a spasso per il mondo con a maglia numero 7 del Portogallo e imitano la sua esultanza.

�Se anche solo una volta vi ho reso felici, se anche solo una volta un mio gol vi ha fatto abbracciare qualcuno, dimenticare una preoccupazione o provare orgoglio per il nostro Paese, allora ne è valsa la pena. Perché essere portoghesi significa anche questo: soffriamo insieme, ci rialziamo insieme. Torniamo a credere. Il Portogallo avrà sempre bisogno di giovani che sognano, di giocatori che non si arrendono mai, di nuove generazioni che credono sia possibile arrivare dove nessuno è mai arrivato. Perché un Paese che continua a creare nuovi eroi è un Paese che non smette mai di credere in se stesso».

«Alla mia famiglia, grazie per aver condiviso con me questo sogno. Grazie per aver compreso le assenze, i sacrifici e i momenti in cui la Nazionale ha dovuto occupare uno spazio che apparteneva anche a voi. Se sono arrivato così lontano è perché siamo sempre stati mano nella mano. E ai portoghesi, a tutti i portoghesi sparsi per il mondo, voglio dire un'ultima cosa: c'è un'immagine che porterò sempre con me, il momento in cui inizia l'inno nazionale».

�La maglia numero 7 resta. L'orgoglio di averla indossata sarà mio per sempre. Il Portogallo sarà sempre dentro di me. Per sempre, Cristiano Ronaldo».

No final, você terá scripts prontos, um checklist de verificação e uma tabela comparativa de precisão × custo dos principais modelos — tudo para identificar e denunciar conteúdos falsos antes que eles influenciem o voto.

Dica prática: se o vídeo contém blink irregular ou sincronização labial “fora do tempo”, pode ser um sinal, mas modelos de 2026 já corrigem esses artefatos.

Se 3 ou mais itens apontarem risco, classifique como “ALTO” e reporte ao Fact‑Checking Hub da sua região.

Os próximos 90 dias serão decisivos para a democracia de três potências. A combinação de vídeos hiper‑realistas, vozes sintéticas e a velocidade de compartilhamento exige uma resposta rápida e baseada em ferramentas acessíveis. Browser Location Access: What It Actually Shares and How to Control It

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

When a website asks "Allow location access?" — most users either click Allow reflexively or Block without fully understanding what they're granting. Here's what browser location access actually means, what extensions can do with it, and how to make deliberate choices.

The browser's Geolocation API lets websites request your location. When a site calls navigator.geolocation.getCurrentPosition(), the browser shows a permission prompt. If you grant it, the browser returns a latitude/longitude coordinate to the page.

On desktop: Not GPS. Desktop browsers use a combination of:

The accuracy on desktop varies from neighborhood-level (Wi-Fi triangulation) to city-level (IP-only). It's not your exact address.

On mobile: GPS is used if available, which is accurate to meters. Mobile location requests are much more precise than desktop.

When you grant location access to a site, the site receives:

That's it — no street address, no "home address," no place names. The website has to reverse geocode the coordinates to get a named location, which is an additional step they may or may not do.

Your IP address is always visible to sites, regardless of location permission. Many sites geolocate from IP before even requesting the Geolocation API.

Browser extensions are different from websites for location access. Extensions don't use the Geolocation API the same way — they have access to browser APIs that websites don't.

What extensions typically do with location: Most legitimate location-related extensions (weather, map, local search) request location to provide their core function — a weather extension needs your location to show weather. This is expected and appropriate.

Red flags: An extension that requests location access but whose stated purpose has nothing to do with location is worth scrutinizing.

Weather & Clock Dashboard for Firefox uses your location to show the weather for your area. The permissions requested are specifically for location and the ability to fetch weather data from an API (Open-Meteo).

The extension doesn't send your location to its own servers. It passes your coordinates to Open-Meteo (the weather data provider) to get forecast data. Open-Meteo is a public API with an open-data license; it doesn't have accounts or persistent tracking.

For users who don't want to share location even with the extension: you can enter a manual location by city name instead, and the extension will use that. No location permission required.

Firefox gives you granular control over location access:

Per-site management: Settings → Privacy & Security → Permissions → Location → Settings. This shows every site that has asked for location access and what you answered. You can change individual site permissions here without affecting others.

Default behavior: You can set Firefox to block all location requests by default, prompt case-by-case (the default), or allow all. Settings → Privacy & Security → Permissions → Location → check "Block new requests asking to access your location" to default to block.

Incognito/private windows: Location permissions granted in a private window don't persist. The site has to ask again next time.

Allow location for: mapping/navigation sites (Google Maps, OpenStreetMap), weather services, local search, delivery sites where location saves you typing your address.

Consider blocking for: news sites, social media platforms, general-purpose shopping sites, sites where your precise location has no clear functional use.

Always review when: a site you don't recognize or haven't used before asks for location, or a site asks for location but you can't figure out why they'd need it.

The permission prompt is there for a reason. Using it deliberately — not reflexively clicking Allow or Block — is the main privacy practice worth building.

Firefox's default geolocation provider is different from Chrome's. Firefox defaults to routing geolocation queries through Mozilla's geolocation service (which uses Google Location Services under the hood but via Mozilla), which provides some additional privacy layer compared to Chrome's direct Google use.

For higher privacy: about:config → geo.provider.network.url can be changed to alternative providers, or geolocation can be disabled entirely by setting geo.enabled to false. This disables the API completely — all location requests will appear to fail to sites.

The practical privacy tradeoff: disabling geolocation entirely means weather extensions, map sites, and local search can't use your location, which is often the reason you're visiting them. The per-site permission model is a better balance for most users than a global block.

Best Firefox Extensions of 2026: The Short List That Actually Matters

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Firefox extension library has hundreds of thousands of entries. Most aren't worth installing. These are the ones that actually make a measurable difference to daily browsing — tested, stable, and genuinely useful.

Install it, leave the default settings on, and forget about it. Advanced mode is available for users who want per-domain blocking control, but the defaults are good.

Who it's for: Anyone who mixes work and personal browsing, developers who test multiple user states, privacy-conscious users.

This is a Firefox-only feature. Chrome doesn't have an equivalent.

What it does: Password manager. Generates, stores, and autofills passwords across devices.

Why not Firefox's built-in: Firefox's built-in password manager is good for most users. Bitwarden adds encrypted vault syncing across all devices (including mobile, other browsers), sharing with family/team, and advanced features like TOTP generation. It's also fully open-source.

Who it's for: Users who access accounts from multiple devices or browsers, teams that need to share credentials.

Free tier is excellent. The paid plan ($10/year) adds emergency access and advanced 2FA.

What it does: Moves your tabs to a vertical sidebar with hierarchical tree structure.

Why it matters: For users who routinely have 20-50+ tabs open, the horizontal tab bar breaks down completely. Tree Style Tab lets you see tab titles in full, groups related tabs under parent pages, and collapses branches when not needed.

Who it's for: Researchers, developers, writers, anyone who maintains many concurrent tab sessions.

The learning curve is real — takes a week to build the habit. The payoff is proportional to how many tabs you typically have open.

What it does: Makes GitHub significantly more usable. Adds PR status indicators, cleaner diff displays, keyboard shortcuts, one-click file navigation, reaction counts on PRs, and dozens of small quality-of-life improvements.

Who it's for: Anyone who uses GitHub regularly for code review, project tracking, or contribution.

The number of small paper cuts it removes is hard to quantify until you use it.

What it does: Learns which domains track you across sites based on observed behavior and blocks them.

Why use it with uBlock Origin: uBlock Origin uses pre-built blocklists. Privacy Badger adds behavioral analysis to catch trackers that aren't on those lists. They complement each other without significant overlap.

Who it's for: Users who care about tracking protection and want a heuristic layer on top of list-based blocking.

What it does: Replaces the default new tab page with time, date, weather forecast, and world clocks.

Why it matters: The default Firefox new tab has a grid of promoted sites and (in some configurations) news/stories content. Neither is useful if you open a new tab to navigate or search. A minimal new tab that shows ambient information — time and weather — without pull content is a better baseline for most workflows.

Weather & Clock Dashboard is the Firefox add-on for this. It shows the current conditions and 3-day forecast using Open-Meteo weather data, plus configurable world clocks for time zone awareness. No feed, no news, no attention design.

Who it's for: Anyone who finds the default new tab distracting, users who check weather during work, remote workers coordinating across time zones.

What it does: Automatically skips sponsored segments, self-promotion, and outros in YouTube videos.

Why it's notable: It's community-crowdsourced — users mark segments in videos, the marks are shared, and the extension skips them for everyone. It works astonishingly well for popular content.

Who it's for: Anyone who watches YouTube regularly and finds the mid-video ads more disruptive than pre-roll.

More extensions = more attack surface and more performance overhead. The list above is the floor of what's worth having for most users, not a shopping cart to fill. Install what solves an actual problem you have; don't install speculatively.

The highest-ROI starting set for most people: uBlock Origin + Containers + a password manager + Weather & Clock Dashboard. That's four extensions, ten minutes to set up, and a meaningfully better browser experience from day one.

Starting from Abbottabad: What Your Hometown Decides — and What It Doesn't

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I am from Abbottabad, Pakistan. It is a small city in the hills, and it is home. Everything I have built — Buzz Capitol, Digi Skill Pro Academy, findservicenest.com, more than fifteen YouTube channels, six books — started there. People sometimes ask me whether starting from a small town held me back. The honest answer is: it decided some things, and it did not decide others. Knowing the difference matters more than the town itself.

Your hometown decides your starting point. It decides what is around you when you are young: the schools, the people you meet, the examples of what a working life can look like. In a small city, you see fewer big companies and fewer obvious paths into media or technology. That is real, and I will not pretend it is not. When I started, there was no local roadmap for what I wanted to do. I had to find the road myself, mostly online.

But your hometown does not decide your reach. The internet changed that equation completely, and it changed it for everyone at once. A student in Abbottabad can take the same free class as a student anywhere else. A reader in another country does not know or care where an article was written. A client hiring a service does not ask for your address before asking whether you can do the job. The work travels; the writer stays put.

That is the first thing my hometown taught me: place is a starting point, not a limit. It taught me to be resourceful, because when the thing you need is not nearby, you learn to find it online, to teach yourself, to ask strangers for help and to help strangers in return. Some of the most useful skills I have came from that habit — looking for answers instead of waiting for them to arrive.

The second thing it taught me is patience. In a small city, things move at their own pace. People know each other. Reputation travels by word of mouth, and it travels slowly — but it also lasts. That shaped how I think about building anything. I did not chase a quick splash. I built steadily, in my own name, one article and one video and one class at a time, because that is how trust works where I come from: you show up, you do the work, and over time people believe you.

There is a part of this story I want to be careful about. I am not saying that where you are from does not matter at all. It matters. It shapes your accent, your manners, your sense of what is possible, and the problems you understand first-hand. I understand what it means to start with limited resources, because I lived it. That understanding is part of why I made Digi Skill Pro Academy's classes completely free. In 2025, more than ten thousand students joined, and more than five thousand of them reported that they started earning. I do not claim those numbers as a triumph of mine; I mention them because they are the reason the academy exists — to give people starting from places like mine a way in.

Starting from Abbottabad also taught me something about independence. When you are far from the centres where decisions get made, you learn to make your own decisions. Buzz Capitol is an independent news and media platform because I wanted it to be free to say what it thinks. That independence was not a strategy I read in a book. It was a habit I learned from building things on my own terms, in a place where nobody was going to build them for me.

I still live and work from here, and I do not see that as a compromise. The world comes to my work through a screen, and my work goes out to the world the same way. My channels reach more than ten million followers across platforms. My books reach readers I will never meet. None of that required me to leave. It required me to stay, and to build something worth finding.

If you are starting from a small town, here is what I would say. Do not waste energy resenting your starting point. Use it. The constraints of a small place teach you to be self-reliant, and self-reliance is the most portable skill there is. Start with what you can do today, with the connection you have. Publish your work under your own name. Let the internet do what it is good at: carrying good work further than its author's address.

Your hometown decides where you begin. It does not decide how far your work can travel. That part is yours.

Sami Ullah is the Founder, CEO and Editor-in-Chief of Buzz Capitol, Chairman of Digi Skill Pro Academy, and author of six books. He lives and works in Abbottabad, Pakistan.

SERP Data Providers Say Google Is Throttling Goto Result Links via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

SERP data providers say Google throttles goto links per results page, so some rank data may store Google redirect links in place of page URLs.

Google is restricting the speed at which anyone can follow (google.com/goto) redirects for search results on each page, as per DataForSEO and Serper. Both sell Google search result API access. Due to this restriction rank data provided by these services may contain Google redirect URLs instead of actual URLs.

According to DataForSEO, the restrictions mean that they have seen some of their request modes only resolving part of the destination URLs. Serper’s status page first noted the issue at 1:30 a.m. UTC on October 30. SerpApi, another seller of search data, also logged goto links for organic results on the same date. Neither DataForSEO nor Serper links to a Google announcement regarding the restriction and the statements below are from their websites.

DataForSEO stated in its November 2nd article that when you open multiple links from the same results page in rapid succession, Google will begin to throttle the redirect(s). Each link carries an identifier based on the results page it originated from.

When Google throttles your redirects, instead of sending your requests for resolution directly to the destination, Google will send back a page with a “Continue” button. That button will only become available after a waiting period. The amount of time the continue button is disabled grows the faster the previous links were resolved.

For example, take a results page that contains 18 goto links to other websites. If all 18 links are requested in quick succession, Google may allow the first 5-6 to be resolved immediately. However, the 7th link may result in a 1 second wait before being able to access the website. The 8th link may then take 3 seconds. And this process continues until the 15th link, where there could be a 40 second wait before accessing that website. DataForSEO reported that they had instances of waiting up to 120 seconds for 1 URL.

The limit is based on the results page and not based on the IP address or the search query used. Also, DataForSEO reports that simply switching IP addresses or search queries will not help bypass this limitation. Additionally, it says that even if you wait an hour or longer before resolving all of the links, Google still uses the same results page limits.

DataForSEO’s Standard Priority mode now resolves all goto URLs, although the added delays may mean execution takes longer. In High Priority and Live modes, the company says about half of goto URLs are resolved.

Serper’s status page says Google “limits how many /goto result links can be followed per results page.” Its first fix resolved the top three organic links and returned results four through 10 as goto links, which Serper said work about 30 seconds after the search. It added a /resolve endpoint to fetch the rest.

On Oct. 31, Serper said it had found another way to get the real URLs and that all result links had them again. It called that “likely temporary.”

SerpApi’s Status Page displays an October 30th event indicating that its Google Search API returned goto links instead of regular links for organic search results; it states that they had the issue fixed by November 2nd. However, there is a second incident reported on November 3rd reporting intermittent issues with the same problem. Neither incident gives a cause.

DataForSEO dates Google’s move to www.google.com/goto links in Search Results to late August. It says the links cannot be decoded, and therefore a tool will have to follow each link to find out where it goes. The link-following part is what the new limit slows down.

Some rank tracking services as well as in house reports using landing pages for URL-level reporting may display Google goto redirects (google.com/goto) in place of true destination URLs from approximately October 30th onward based upon the service provider and how the requests were made. If a specific URL is missing from a URL-level report it could still be showing up in a ranking position and have an unresolved redirect at the URL location. Prior to reporting a URL-level loss check whether the stored URL is a Google redirect, also contact your service provider and determine what method was used to collect the data.

As of my check on Nov. 4, SerpApi’s Incident Report on goto links from Nov. 3 is still listed as Identified. Serper states that in the event that Google also puts a cap on the method it uses for retrieving actual URLs, it would revert to automatically resolving the top three results and use /resolve to fetch the remaining after approximately thirty seconds. DataForSEO stated it will continue to adjust its processing based on what it observes regarding how Google deals with goto resolutions.

Reports covering the last few days of October through early November should be reviewed again at some point when providers have determined how they will handle goto resolution.

Follow on YouTube. Matt G. Southern is the Senior News Writer at Search Engine Journal, where he’s covered Google, SEO, ...

In a world ruled by algorithms, SEJ brings timely, relevant information for SEOs, marketers, and entrepreneurs to optimize and grow their businesses -- and careers.

Nvidia Investors Need to Be Paying Attention on Nov. 17

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mark your calendar. Nvidia is on the verge of a business update that will be incredibly important for the stock market.

Nvidia (NVDA -3.17%) is on the verge of a crucial moment. On Nov. 17, it will publish its third-quarter earnings results, and this event has huge implications for the whole stock market.

As the leading provider of the high-powered processing hardware used to train and power today's most advanced artificial intelligence (AI) models, Nvidia has become the single most important company to watch when it comes to the health of both the AI industry and the broader market. That means that its upcoming report could spur substantial movement in the company's share price, and could also have a reverberating impact on many other holdings in investors' portfolios.

Stock valuations in the AI industry have experienced an incredible run-up, but investors have recently been questioning the sustainability of their growth-dependent valuations. Along with macroeconomic dynamics, the market is wavering on AI-specific dynamics, including monetization efficiency, financing, and electricity sourcing. Nvidia's stock price has pulled back as these concerns have intensified, and it's now down roughly 13.5% from the high that it reached last month. In this environment, the company's upcoming report is poised to be an important valuation test.

With the Q2 report that it published at the end of August, Nvidia issued strong guidance for the third quarter. Sales were forecast to grow by roughly 106% year over year to approximately $108 billion. The company also said that it expected a non-GAAP (adjusted) gross margin of roughly 74%.

Nvidia's history of big earnings and revenue beats is creating high expectations for its third-quarter report. Simply hitting the company's guidance targets or equaling the average Wall Street analyst estimates would probably provoke a bearish response. Investors are looking for the company to deliver strong indications that demand for advanced AI processors and related technologies remains sky-high. With AI stocks faltering recently, Nov. 17 won't just be an important date to watch for Nvidia investors -- it will be an important day in determining where the stock market heads next.

Keith Noonan has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Nvidia. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

He Trolls AI Labs for a Living. Now They All Want to Work With Him

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

OpenAI, Anthropic, and xAI have all asked creator Matt Husk to collaborate following the viral success of his videos trolling AI models.

During the WIRED World Fair on Wednesday, Husk told the crowd “all the big AI companies” have inquired about working with him, including Elon Musk’s xAI, which Husk claimed offered a Tesla for a week if he made videos exclusively for the firm.

The comedian has gained some 2 million followers combined across Instagram, TikTok, and YouTube, as well as a deal with the talent agency Unicorn, through videoing himself making deadpan requests to ChatGPT’s voice model—asking it to count to 100, or to beatbox, for example. His viral videos have exposed the weaknesses of OpenAI’s LLM, which comically botches the tasks and swings between sycophancy and gaslighting Husk, and earned him the title of “Sam Altman’s worst nightmare.”

In April, Altman, nodding and smiling, admitted on tech podcast Mostly Human that the model’s inability to set an accurate timer was a “known issue” and would be for another year, after being shown a Husk video in which he asked ChatGPT to time him running a mile and found that it measured a span of a few seconds as lasting “around 10 minutes.” When Husk says “I swear I was faster than that,” it replies with a sympathetic tone: “It can feel faster in the moment.”

During his interview at WIRED World Fair with Shell Game podcast host Evan Ratliff, Husk said OpenAI offered him early access to a patch for the timer issue if he shared his account. He declined. The company had previously emailed Husk asking to collaborate on a video. “They offered me the smallest amount,” he said. “I was just like, ‘No.’” The firm then offered to send him gifts and asked for his home address; Again, Husk declined.

OpenAI, Anthropic, and xAI (which was recently rolled into SpaceX to create SpaceXAI and then SpaceXSI after President Trump renamed artificial intelligence “super intelligence”) did not respond to requests for comment.

AI labs are increasingly pitching their products as capable of handling everyday chores. On Tuesday, OpenAI announced its model had scored “very highly” on a benchmark built to model its ability to help people in their personal lives, such as meal prep and enrolling a child in kindergarten. “The way they promote it is like ‘It can do anything,’” Husk said, “so I’ll put it to the test—see if it can order bread for me.” In one of his videos, Husk asks AI to help him negotiate some bread on Craigslist down from a starting price of $10,000 to $5. The model accepts a price of $400.

Husk began publicly trolling chatbots about a year ago. He told Ratliff, a WIRED contributor, at the time he liked to make TikToks asking people questions, but there weren’t many around while he was working his landscaping job, and he ran out of ideas. So he recorded himself telling ChatGPT a nuclear bomb had gone off. The model’s response, “I’m rooting for you,” and advice to take shelter “was not helpful at all,” he said. “I thought it was a pretty bad video,” he told Ratliff, “and the next morning I had 100,00 views, and I was like, ‘Let’s tell it I got hit by a train.’”

Since then, Husk has secured brand partnerships with CeraVe and Ninja. He also has an upcoming original series with The Onion, where he learns complicated skills, like being a mime, over Zoom. “I'm spending too much time talking to AI,” Husk explained, “so I'm going to branch out and learn stuff from humans.”

While Husk finds himself talking to AI a lot to scope out ideas for videos, “I don't really use it in that creative sense,” such as to generate ideas, he said, because they would all be “too similar.” “I'd rather just have my worst idea at least be my idea rather than AI's idea for a video,” he said.
